# alarm-clock.org — Progress Log

**The site:** alarm-clock.org is a free, no-sign-up static site on Cloudflare
Pages offering browser-based clock tools — an alarm clock (with full-screen
bedside mode and multiple saved alarms), a countdown timer, a stopwatch, a
world clock — plus shareable countdowns whose entire definition lives in the
URL. The whole public site is generated by `seo/tools/*.mjs` from `seo/_data/*`
via `npm run build`; the generated `*/index.html` directories are disposable
output. See `CLAUDE.md` for the architecture and working rules. Deploy = push to
`main` (Cloudflare auto-publishes).

---
### 2026-08-30 — One Moon engine, ten testable questions

- NEW `/moon-simulator/?state=<name>`: ten stable string states choose a distinct
  question, task, control, first position, observation and shareable URL.
- The same build-time painter and hashed deferred controller now power ten Moon
  concept pages; the repeated generic Earth-orbit image is gone from them.
- Every experiment ships a complete SVG, task and result before JavaScript, and
  `check-moon-lab` gates URL-safe states, finite frames and one-to-one concepts.
- `/questions/` is now “Questions you can test”: experiments first, then concise
  answers grouped by phenomenon; the weak “Big questions” hierarchy is retired.
- Moon and questions CSS are separately probed route sections; clocks, timers,
  unrelated concepts and the questions directory pay no unused Moon Lab CSS.
- Canonical, delivery and session rules are now measured guidelines: unique
  intent decides canonical; critical CSS stays inline; shared JS is deferred.
- Full page/link/astronomy checks and desktop/mobile browser QA passed; the only
  existing advisories are the repository's dated-event verification reminders.

  what each would cover — instead of a catalog.
- GUIDED CONTENT STAYS (owner: "good quality... inspires curiosity"): the
  day/night map's Things to try, the orbit page's Four true things, hub
  questions. Each such card now ends with a door: "help us turn it into a

- NEW concept /concepts/who-invented-time/ ("nobody — people read it off the

  and FAQ. docs/IDEAS.md created — the owner's parking lot (space-created-
  time full treatment, the scientists series, and more).

The live page drew an ellipse around empty space with the Sun parked off to

middle but auto-zoomed so Slow down looked like nothing happened, and drew
the velocity arrow the opposite way of the dashed “no gravity” line.

The page is now a small lesson: Sun stays put, you change the speed on the
right, the other side of the path is what moves. Play / Circle / Slow down /

A faint original circle stays on screen so the change is visible. Gold always
points at the Sun; green is always sideways, in the direction of motion.

### 2026-08-24 (4) — the equator, and the four corners of the year

Owner's question, and the physics checks out: the subsolar marker's LATITUDE is
the sun's declination, so it already sat north of the equator through northern
summer and south through northern winter — verified against the series (today
+10.9°, June +23.4°, December −23.4°). What was missing was the reference line
to see it against.

**The shared map (home hero + the world-clock card on /time/ and /earth/) now
draws the equator and both tropics**, latitudes taken from the same solar
series that draws the night side. The tropics are the band the sun can never
leave, which is the tilt made visible.

**Four season buttons on the home hero** — March equinox, June solstice,
September equinox, December solstice — jump the DATE while keeping the time of
day (DAY0 is the anchor midnight the slider scrubs; Now returns it to today).
The instants are baked from a new shared `seasonPoints()` in daynight.mjs,
which /day-night-map/'s own YEAR scan now also uses, so the two pages cannot
disagree about a solstice or where the tropics are.

**A read-out sentence on both pages says what the latitude MEANS** — "the sun
is overhead at 23.4° S — south of the equator, so the southern half of the
world is having its summer, and Antarctica is in daylight around the clock" —
with an equinox phrased as an equinox rather than as a 0.0°-south summer.
Deliberately worded so it cannot teach the common error the owner's question
raised: half the planet is lit on every date; the tilt decides which half gets
the bigger share, not how much is lit.

Files: daynight.mjs (seasonPoints), build-daynight.mjs, build-home.mjs,
11-home.css.

### 2026-08-24 (3) — the hero map becomes an instrument

Owner's mid-flight asks, all on the landing hero. **The overhead sun** is now
drawn on the map (baked at build, moved to the real minute by the shared map
script, which also learned to stand down when the slider holds the map). **A
slider under the map scrubs today**: it opens at now and follows the clock;
drag it and the night side, the sun dot AND the four city clocks all repaint
for that minute of your local day, the label reads "6:00 AM your time", and a
Now chip hands the map back to the clock (window.__wkHold is the truce flag
between the slider and the everywhere-else auto-tick).

**Location, without a modal**: a small pin button rides the map's top-right
corner — "See your location". A deliberate press triggers the browser's own
permission prompt, nothing of ours; granted, a "You" dot lands on the map, the
button retires, and the coordinates live only in this browser under the same
dn_home key the /day-night-map/ page uses, so the two share one answer. No
geolocation on the device → the button never shows. **And the bonus worked:**
the orrery tile re-draws with the shared coordinates, so once located, the
visitor watches the moon circle an Earth with THEIR OWN town riding the turn,
labelled You.

Verified in Chromium: scrub + label + Now, grant path (dot at Paradise, CA,
orrery carrying You), deny/absent paths, zero page errors. Files:
build-home.mjs, 11-home.css.

Three owner asks. **Collaboration is now a primary task of the page**: the

owner's example — "Why is the night sky so dark?" — which required the site to
be able to answer it: /questions/ gained an eighth card (#dark-sky, Olbers'
paradox) that first separates the easy half (the Earth turned you away from
the sun → day/night map) from the deep half (in an infinite eternal universe

has an age). The chain rethreads through it and the closer is now "the ninth
question is yours".

**The site's own animations, small, on the landing page**: Earth and Saturn
as live fourth cards in their sections' grids (pc-grid-4), turning via the
SAME GLOBE_JS engine the planet pages use — first frame baked at build,
engine hoisted to a cached file, reduced-motion and hidden-tab mannered,
rotation hours from satRotation() not typed. Saturn's rings open at the real
angle for the date, on the home page, spinning.

Two escaping traps caught by browser testing, again: \n inside a template
literal is a real newline in the emitted JS (broke the form script), and a
${} in a single-quoted string doesn't interpolate (Saturn's caption printed
its own source). Both fixed; every inline script on the page now parses.

### 2026-08-23 (4) — the lesson template, proven on Seasons 7–8

Big audit item #1, done as an EXEMPLAR: one lesson carries the full template
before any other is touched, so a real teacher can react to the pattern before
it is copied fourteen times.

**The template, as optional lesson fields the renderer picks up:** `launch`
(the "Teach this tomorrow" card, ABOVE all prose — prep, materials, a
one-projector-no-devices line, standards, vocabulary, the misconception, the
engagement mechanic — with three working buttons: copy-projector-link,
open-student-view, print), `mission` (the hour in the students' own terms, as
a pullquote), three-part `steps` (teacher does / students do, the split
rendered as a second line per row), `paths` (support ≈35min / core / extension
+ six access lines: ELL, reading, visual, motor, hearing, screen reader),
`artifact` (the claim-evidence-reasoning paragraph every student leaves with),
and `assess` (success criteria, two mid-lesson checks, a three-question exit
ticket with the answer key in a closed <details> so a projected page cannot
leak it). Lessons without the fields render exactly as before.

**The student view** — /classroom/lessons/<slug>/student/, written for any
lesson with mission+assess: the same hour with the teacher's half removed.
Mission, what-YOU-do rows, the exit ticket without answers, a name line.
noindex (companion, not competitor). This is also the answer to the audit's
K–2 finding — children never see the teacher page at all.

**Print IS the download.** A sitewide @media print reset in 02-base flips the
dark theme to black-on-white, drops the chrome (nav, menus, forms, buttons),
and keeps write-on rules — so the lesson page prints as the teacher one-pager
and the student view prints as the worksheet, with no PDF pipeline to
maintain. Verified in Chromium print emulation.

Rollout queue (1x1, next sessions): the other four seasons bands, then moon
phases, then solar system — rewriting the shared age-band passages as each
band is touched. Files: build-classroom.mjs, 14-worldclock.css (lp-*),
02-base.css (print), build-inline.mjs (student pages registered).

### 2026-08-23 (3) — the two small audit items: a page budget, and no stored IPs

**Performance budget, in the gate that already runs last.** check-pages now
fails any emitted page over 400KB raw. The largest legitimate page is
/planets/ at ~292KB (the landing page fell from 318KB to 115KB when the tabs
became pages), so the ceiling is a third above reality — what it exists to
catch is an injection bug bloating all 4,000 self-contained pages silently,
which is exactly how the multiplying footer behaved. Runs in every build and
in CI, so it cannot be skipped.

**No raw IP is stored anywhere any more.** The keyed HMAC tag moved from
views.js into a shared functions/api/_iptag.js, and now covers all three
consumers: the view counter, purpose.js's daily key, and — the audit's actual
finding — report.js, which had been keeping the raw address inside every
90-day report record and mailing it to the owner. The address is now read,
tagged and dropped: the rate limit, the stored record and the email all carry
the pseudonym, which still rate-limits and still groups repeat abuse, which
is all the address was for. Falls back to the old unkeyed hash until
VIEW_HASH_KEY is set (still pending, owner).

Files: check-pages.mjs, functions/api/_iptag.js (new), views.js, report.js,
purpose.js.

### 2026-08-23 — an outside review, triaged: two real bugs, four fixes, three rebuttals

An external (ChatGPT) audit arrived. Every factual claim was verified against
the code before acting; two were real bugs, several were sound hardening, and
three big recommendations contradict deliberate architecture and were declined
with reasons.

**Bug 1 — report.js used `isIdea` without defining it (their find).** The
definition existed in history and was lost in the emailOptional refactor. With
REPORT_WEBHOOK or Resend configured, every submission threw a ReferenceError:
the Sheet still got the report (that fetch is queued first) but the submitter
saw a server error, and the KV backstop and the email never ran. Restored as
`const isIdea = emailOptional`.

**Bug 2 — the footer multiplied (their find, my bug).** injectSiteLinks
inserts TWO paragraphs and stripped old ones with a NON-GLOBAL regex, so every
build left one extra pair on the hand-maintained pages (regenerated pages never
showed it). privacy.html had reached THIRTEEN copies. Strips are global now,
injectCopyright's too, and **check-pages gates the counts** (sitelinks ≤ 2,
copyright ≤ 1, trademark ≤ 1) so this class of bug fails the build. Verified
idempotent: two consecutive build-inline runs differ only by the build stamp.

**Hardening done:** the legacy `?key=` admin form on /api/reports is retired
(header + localStorage is the supported route — a secret in a URL lands in
history and logs). The view counter's IP tag is now HMAC-SHA-256 keyed with
VIEW_HASH_KEY, truncated to 12 hex chars — an unkeyed 32-bit hash of an IPv4
is a lookup table away from the address; falls back to the old hash until the
owner sets the env var (Pages -> Settings -> Environment variables).

**Landmarks (their find was real, the cause was ours):** build-inline's
role="main" injection string-matched '<div class="wrap">' exactly, missing
every wrap-wide page — the home page included. Now a regex over any wrap
variant, plus id="main" and a skip link injected on every page (skip-link CSS
in 02-base, core).

**CI:** their claim of "no registered workflows" was WRONG (four, all active),
but the useful half stood: no per-push gate. .github/workflows/ci.yml now runs
npm ci + npm run build (which ends in all four gates) + check:solar on every
push to main and every PR.

**Declined, with reasons:** externalising CSS/JS (the per-section inlining is
deliberate — a page ships only the CSS its markup can use, and zero
render-blocking requests is the architecture, not an accident); un-committing
generated HTML (CLAUDE.md's reproducibility contract); splitting the home page
(the tab row already is that split). Real but deferred as projects: the lesson
template overhaul (launch card / mission / minute-by-minute / three pathways /
exit ticket — the best idea in the review), age-banded rewrites of shared
lesson passages, CSP nonces to drop 'unsafe-inline', converting teacher
submissions into a PII-free public backlog.

Also: 8 remote branches fully behind main should be deleted (session creds got
403 on branch deletion — owner one-click); 5 diverged branches hold 10 unique
commits, mostly superseded, the Intl.DateTimeFormat-caching one worth a look.

### 2026-08-21 — /questions/, the curiosity chain

Owner's brief: a student should be able to arrive holding nothing but a
question — why don't the planets fall into the sun? why seasons? why phases? —
and navigate page to page from curiosity alone. The answers already existed
across the simulators; what was missing was a door shaped like the question.

**/questions/ is that door, and it is a chain, not a list.** Seven questions
(fall in / fly away, gravity+motion→orbit, change one part, seasons, phases,
tides, Jupiter's moons), each answered honestly in two paragraphs, each ending
by literally handing the reader the next question, and the last handing them
the standing offer: the eighth question is yours, send it, and a class that
does is credited (→ work-with-us). Every answer carries "see it happen" chips
into the simulator that tests it, so the page routes as well as explains.

**Every figure is derived**: the tilt from the daynight solar series (23.4),
the speeds from the sun's GM + the element table check-planets proves (47.9 /
29.8 / 5.4 km/s), Jupiter = 318x Earth from the two GMs check-solar-data
enforces, and the moon's 29.5-day cycle via a new orrSpanDays export from
orrery.mjs rather than a second copy of the constant. Speeds in the prose are
units-aware (verified in Chromium: 47.9 km/s → 29.7 mi/s on the menu toggle);
the FAQPage JSON-LD keeps metric. The seven Q&As double as FAQPage LD, which
is exactly the query shape these questions have in search.

Routes in: a new drawn question-mark icon leads the space block in the nav,
the footer's topic line now opens with "the big questions", the home guides
line opens with "Start with a question", and /about/, /about/work-with-us/,
/classroom-adjacent orbital + day/night "keep going" rows all link it.

### 2026-08-19 (9) — a forever-words audit, and the sponsor shape the owner wants

Swept every claim on /about/, /about/work-with-us/ and /sponsors/ for absolutes.
The rule that came out of it, now in CLAUDE.md: the site's commitments come in
**two kinds and are written differently**.

The ETHICAL floor stays absolute, because it costs nothing and it is the point:
nothing about a student is ever published, nothing tracks or profiles a child, a
page a ten-year-old is reading is not a shop, and no sponsorship brings
third-party JavaScript with it (rescoped from "no third-party JavaScript, ever"
to "none comes with a sponsorship … not a thing I will sell, at any price",
which is a promise about deals rather than about the future of the web).

The BUSINESS claims are now today-plus-intention. "Everything free today stays
free" became "what I intend for as long as I am running the site … if that ever
had to change, this page would say so, in advance". "Nothing to pay, ever"
became "there is nothing to pay". A class credit that stood "for as long as the
page exists" now stands on the page "and is carried across if that page is ever
rebuilt" — the same kindness without the eternal clause.

**And the sponsorship shape changed to what the owner actually wants:** one
sponsor, sitewide, for the LONG TERM, **structured so growth pays** — a base
plus steps tied to real traffic or milestones, so a sponsor has a reason to help
it reach more classrooms. Reviewed once a year against the numbers whatever the
length; a section sponsorship still available where the fit is obvious; a credit
stands while the agreement does. Long agreement welcome, permanence not for
sale.

Also fixed a stale line while auditing: /countdown/'s sub still advertised
"celebrity birthdays … big public parties", two categories retired in the
classroom pass.

### 2026-08-19 (7) — /sponsors/, and an ask on the About page

The other half of the funding standard: a page a funder can actually read.
/sponsors/ sets out what the site is (4,000+ pages, 1,100+ towns, 165 tide
stations, every figure computed), what a sponsor gets (a plain "supported by"
credit, ideally on a specific tool they funded, beside the class whose idea it
was; a page about them; an annual account of what the money built), and — the
part that makes the rest worth anything — **what a sponsor cannot buy**: a
number, a conclusion, editorial placement, anything about a student, a tracking
pixel, or exclusivity over the subject.

**The technical terms are part of the offer, not a preference:** a credit is
static HTML built in at build time, no third-party JavaScript ever, a logo is an
inlined SVG served from this domain (so a sponsor's own analytics never sees a
visitor here), and the site stays prebuilt and fast. GA remains the only third
party.

It also says out loud that most of the money in this field can only go to a
nonprofit or an institution, and that a fiscal sponsor, museum, district or
university partnership is welcome — a structure problem worth solving rather
than a reason not to apply.

**No company is named as a target.** The page describes categories of
organisation, which is honest and keeps the site clear of implying an
affiliation it does not have. The named-company work belongs in a private
pitch list, not on the site.

/about/#funding now ends with the ask and links here; /sponsors/ is in the
sitemap and the "where to start" chips. Deliberately NOT in the sitewide
footer: a sponsorship pitch under 4,000 pages would undercut the thing being
sold. Files: sponsors/index.html (new), about/index.html,
about/work-with-us/index.html, build-inline.mjs, build-sitemap.mjs.

### 2026-08-19 (6) — stop promising "no advertising, ever"

Owner's call, and the right one: the site may need to pay for itself one day —
most likely a sponsor — and two lines were writing cheques he might not be able
to cash. /about/work-with-us/ said "Free, permanently … there is no business
model here to change", and /about/ said "no advertising … anywhere on the site"
under a heading that read as a forever promise.

Both now describe the PRESENT and link to one new section, **/about/#funding**,
which is the only place that speaks for the site on money. It says plainly that
nothing pays for it today, that this may change, and then commits to a STANDARD
rather than a ban: tools stay free with no account; nothing tracks or profiles
children; nothing is sold to students; anything paid for is labelled as paid
for where it appears; a sponsor buys a name and a thank-you, never a number or
a conclusion; and that page changes first if any of it changes. It closes by
naming the kinds of organisation that would fit and inviting the argument if
someone thinks the standard is too weak.

That reads better to a teacher than "never any ads" did, because it is checkable
— every figure on the site being computed is what makes "nobody buys what a
page says" a testable claim instead of a slogan.

ask the class what it should do next, send the idea, and **the class is credited

survive only on countdown-family pages (EVENT_PAGE), which are the ones that
need them. The home page's "Can't find it? Suggest a birthday, holiday or
event" line went with them.

gravity, motion, time and space as one set of rules, with each page named as one

link", which stopped being true in the previous pass. Signed.

**The asteroid belt page** gained the point it was missing: the belt does not
fall into the sun because every rock in it is moving sideways at the speed its
distance demands — 20.7 km/s at the inner edge, 16.5 at the outer, against
Earth's 29.8 — and stopping one dead would drop it into the sun 192 days later.
All four figures derived from the sun's GM and the belt edges, which are
themselves derived from Jupiter's orbit. It also says the belt does not rotate
as a wheel: 3.0 years inner against 5.9 outer is the shear that makes the
resonances matter. Links to the orbital-velocity simulator, whose URL now lives
in solar-pages.mjs so the two cannot drift.

Home lede + meta description now lead with gravity, motion, time and space.

### 2026-08-19 (3) — the map's buttons: taller, and location asks for itself

Owner's follow-ups on /day-night-map/. Now and Play are 40px tall rather than
chip-height (they are pressed on a phone, over a picture, and from across a
room on a projector), and a third button joined them BESIDE Now: a drawn pin
glyph plus "My location", the label dropping below 520px where the map cannot
spare the width. No emoji — the pin is an inline SVG in currentColor.

**The location is asked for on load, and the button is only the fallback.**
Three states, all verified in Chromium: permission granted -> the dot appears
and the button stays hidden, because it would only repeat what already
happened; refused or timed out -> the button appears (silently on the
automatic attempt; a deliberate press earns an explanation); no geolocation on
the device at all -> the button is removed from the DOM, since a control that
can never do anything is worse than no control. A location granted on an
earlier visit resolves with no prompt, so the common case is silent.

### 2026-08-19 (2) — /day-night-map/, and two things that were quietly broken

**The new page.** The home page's day-and-night card now carries two links —
World clock, and Day/night map — side by side on one line, and the card shows
on the Time tab AND the Earth tab. The map's own page is the same picture with
time attached: Now and Play sitting ON the map's bottom edge (left and right,
as asked), a slider across the next seven days under it, jump buttons for the
solstices and equinoxes, a My-location dot, and the four world-clock cities
showing their local time and whether they are in daylight at the instant on
show. Then the writing: how to read the map, why the line leans (and what the
tilt has to do with it), and why a flat map is honest about time and dishonest
about size — with the stretch factor at each latitude computed, not asserted,
and Greenland against Africa as the punchline.

**The drawing moved into `daynight.mjs`**, shared with the home card, and the
old sliding-group trick went with it. The card used to bake the terminator
relative to its own subsolar meridian and translate it, with a copy either side
to cover the wrap; that is invisible at one repaint a minute and jumps a whole
map width the moment you press Play. The shading is now SOLVED per meridian in
absolute coordinates — verified over 40 frames of playback, the path spans
exactly x=0..720 every frame — so there is nothing to slide and nothing to
wrap. Twilight arrived for free: the same solver at -18 degrees, drawn evenodd
against the 0-degree curve, gives a real dusk band instead of a hard edge.

**`check-daynight.mjs` found two bugs the eye could not.** It proves the
shading against the altitude formula at ~850,000 points, and the first run
failed: a root came back as 275 degrees when it meant -85 and was thrown away
as out of range (shading a lit meridian dark near the equinoxes), and `asin`
was being handed 1.0000000000000002 at the subsolar point, which is NaN. Both
fixed; the solver now returns the dark part of a meridian as up to three
pieces, including the middle BAND you get at an equinox when both poles are in
twilight. That case is now visibly, correctly drawn.

**Ten live pages were serving merge-conflict markers.** `<<<<<<< HEAD` /
`=======` / `>>>>>>> origin/main`, empty on both sides, sitting in the `<head>`
of /about/, /privacy, /404, /c.html, /not-found, /how-it-works,
/browser-limitations, /report/, /suggest-event/ and /stopwatch/. The parser
treats the stray text as body content and closes `<head>` early, so every
visitor read a line of conflict markers above the logo — spotted by the owner
on a phone. Removed, and `check-pages` now fails the build on any conflict
marker in an emitted page: the title, body, description and nav checks all
passed those ten pages happily.

### 2026-08-16 (3) — the rest of the units, and "what if a moon slowed down?"

**Units, finished.** Everything the last entry listed as outstanding now follows
the menu:
- the orbital simulator's live read-outs, through `window.acFmt` — the same
  conversion and rounding the spans use, reached as a function, for figures a
  script writes rather than the build. Its prose and table went to spans; the
  meta description and the FAQ (which becomes JSON-LD) stay plain text, because
  a span in either is a bug.
- the classroom's corridor walk — 16 mm marble, peppercorn moon, the sun down
  the hall — in mm, cm and m.
- tide heights. NOAA is asked for feet, so the page holds feet and asks
  `acFromFt` for the reader's version: every tile, chip, table row, chart label,
  tooltip and the CSV header, redrawn when the choice changes.
- the moon page's nearby-city dropdown, which can hold no markup at all, so it
  formats with acFmt and rebuilds on `ac:units`.
- temperatures. They live in prose ("−110 °C at the cloud tops"), so `temps()`
  wraps the FIGURE and leaves the sentence alone — applied to the planet pages'
  temperature row and facts, and to /planets/'s overview paragraphs, after
  escaping and never in a JSON-LD or meta path.

**"What if a moon slowed down?"** — a new Space card on the home page (owner's
ask). One planet, one moon, Slow it down / Speed it up / Circle. The picture is
the honest two-body answer rather than an animation of a guess: give a circular
orbit speed k times the circular speed and the vis-viva equation gives
a = r/(2−k²) and e = |k²−1|, with the push point held at aphelion when it is
slowed and perihelion when it is sped up — so the ellipse pivots about the moon
instead of sliding — and the moon sweeps equal areas in equal times, which is
why it visibly crawls at the far end. ~40 lines, no imports. Checked in a
browser: slowed to k = 0.76 the moon's distance runs 70 → 29 → 70, aphelion
exactly where the change was made.

**And the page it links to gained the prose to match:** "What could actually
speed a planet up or slow it down?" (tides, drag, resonances, close passes, the
sun's mass loss, Yarkovsky — every one of them a change in orbital ENERGY, and
none of them a spiral into the sun) and "What if something big hit it?" (the
momentum ledger, why the direction of the blow decides the shape, why an impact
changes spin far more easily than orbit, and DART as the one case where the
mass ratio runs the other way).

### 2026-08-16 (2) — metric or imperial, chosen once, in the menu

Owner: most measurements are in kilometres; detect the country, use what that
country uses, and let the reader choose above Projector mode.

**The mechanism** is seo/tools/units.mjs. Every figure is emitted once, in
metric, inside a span carrying the raw value, the unit and the precision it was
written to; the script that rides with the nav rewrites those spans for an
imperial reader and back again. No second copy of any number, nothing to fetch,
and the shipped HTML is still complete for a crawler or a no-JS visitor.

**The default is the browser's locale, not an IP lookup** — the language a
reader has set is a statement about how they want to be addressed; an edge
location is a guess about where their traffic exits, and one of those is free.
US, LR and MM are the three imperial-distance countries; everything else gets
metric, en-GB included (British road signs are miles, British science is not),
and a stored choice always wins. The control is Metric | Imperial in the menu,
directly above Projector mode.

**Rounding travels with the figure.** 139,820 km written to five significant
figures must not come back as eleven in miles, so the span carries `data-s` and
the converter rounds to match.

**Converted so far:** /planets/ (widths, belt, the AU), all eleven planet pages
(widths, every moon's distance and diameter, the big-asteroid and
planet-comparison tables), the rocket-launch pages' delta-v figures, the
supermoon and blue-moon pages, and the /sun/ and /moon/ place pages' nearest-city
distance and elevation. Two pages that printed BOTH systems side by side — the
supermoon list and the elevation row — now print one, because the menu answers
the question they were hedging.

**Not converted yet, and still metric-only:** the orbital-velocity simulator's
live read-outs (its own script writes them each frame — it needs to ask
window.acUnits() and listen for the ac:units event), the classroom and simulator
marble-walk figures in mm/m, tide heights (client-rendered from NOAA), the moon
page's nearby-city dropdown (JS-built, in miles), and temperatures, which live
in solar-facts.json as prose ("−110 °C at the cloud tops") rather than as
numbers. The converter already knows how to do °C→°F; the figures need pulling
out of the sentences first.

Files: seo/tools/units.mjs (new), build-inline.mjs, icons.mjs, place.mjs,
build-planets.mjs, build-solar.mjs, build-moon-events.mjs,
assets/css/parts/02-base.css, CLAUDE.md.

Both done, in the shared component, so /sun/ and /moon/ move together.

**The bar was the wrong unit.** It covered a DAY — 1,439 minutes — and what the
picture draws is the moon's angle from the sun, which takes 29 days 13 hours to
come round. The whole length of the control bought a thirtieth of the one cycle
it exists to show, which is why the moon looked pinned however far you dragged.
Max is now ORR_SPAN_MIN (42,524 minutes = one synodic month), anchored at
midnight of the day the card is on and re-anchored whenever the day is chosen
elsewhere. Verified: dragging to the right-hand edge lands 29 days on with the
moon back within a few pixels of where it started — one lap, exactly.

**Dragging now crosses days**, which the host page's per-day work cannot follow
per frame, so `input` moves the picture alone and `change` — once, on release —
hands the page the day it landed on. Play wraps inside the same span, so the two
controls are one journey.

**The stamp row is now field · Play · Now.** The label is gone from the markup
and the CSS; the sentence under the bar carries the state instead.

### 2026-08-13 (6) — Play on the sun and moon place pages

Owner: the orrery card on /sun/ and /moon/ city pages should be able to run,
and the city spins while the moon stays put.

Both true. There was no Play button at all — only the date field, Now and the
day slider — and the slider is scoped to ONE day, across which the moon travels
13 degrees. So the card showed a city whirling round an Earth the moon appeared
to be pinned to, which is the opposite of the thing it is drawn to show.

**Play, beside Now, above the slider**, on both families (one component, so one
change). It runs at one drawn day every 1.4 seconds: a full lunar cycle takes
about 41 seconds and the city marker comes round every 1.4, fast enough to read
as spin and slow enough to follow. While it runs, every frame calls orrSet with
dayChanged FALSE — so the host page repaints the picture and its live rows and
NOT the day's own facts (sunrise, the dial, the 7-day table), which would be a
full re-render sixteen times a second. The page catches up in one re-render the
moment it stops. Now, the date field, the slider and hiding the tab all stop it.

Verified in a headless browser on both pages: the instant advances ~2.1 days in
3 s, the moon's drawn position moves with it, the heading switches to "Where
the moon will be", Pause holds, and Now returns the card to the live clock.

**Merge note.** Another session shipped a Play button on main while this one was
being written (f7c5151007), and it answers a different question: it walks the
slider through ONE day on a loop, which keeps the host page's per-day work out
of the frame loop but leaves the moon creeping its real 13° — the exact thing
the owner then reported ("the city spins, the moon does not"). This branch's
version wins the merge: Play is beside Now above the slider, and it crosses
days, so the lunar cycle is visible. Their two good ideas are kept — `.orr-spin`
takes the city's name and leader line off while the picture moves, and the line
under the slider doubles as the card's state read-out.

### 2026-08-13 (5) — no simulator on a planet with no moons

**Mercury and Venus were being given the solar system simulator.** Their pages
have no moon system to draw, so `rung` fell through to the inner-planets view:
the whole drawing engine — orbits, rungs, share builder, ~55KB of script —
loaded onto a page about one planet, to show that planet as one dot among four.
It answered a question the page was not asking. Both now carry the turning
globe and a link, and nothing in between; their script is 45KB of globe
animator and their titles no longer say "Simulator", because there isn't one.
The URLs keep the -simulator suffix: those two pages are indexed at them and a
rename would cost that for a word. Every planet WITH moons keeps its simulator
— that picture is its own system and exists nowhere else on the site.

`pageJs` gained a `sim` flag that omits the simulator IIFE entirely rather than
shipping it to run against elements that are not there, and `page()` skips
assertNeeds for those pages, since they open at no rung.

**A latent hoisting bug, found by making two scripts identical.** hoistShared
keyed its written-file map by content hash alone, while the filename carries
the page's name — so two families whose scripts happen to be byte-identical
wrote ONE file, under whichever name came first, and the other family's pages
kept pointing at a URL nothing had written. Venus 404'd on its own script for
exactly one build. Keyed by name AND hash now, and the two globe-only pages
share an honestly named `sol-globe-only` file instead of carrying a planet
name each.

**"Everything about Mercury" was overclaiming** on /planets/ (owner). The
planet pages are good but they are not everything. "More about X" now, on the
cards and on the rocket-launch destination link that used the same phrase.

### 2026-08-13 (4) — full screen actually makes the picture bigger

Two owner reports on the solar pages, both right.

**Full screen barely changed the size of the drawing.** The cause: it stacked
the read-out panel UNDER the picture and capped it at 27vh. On a 1080-tall
screen that panel and its gaps took ~330px, so the square could only reach
~700 — against the 756 (70vh) the page itself already allows. Full screen was
making the picture smaller than the card. The drawing is square, so height is
the only dimension that can grow it, and a square in a 16:9 frame leaves ~890px
of width doing nothing. Landscape now puts the picture in column one, top to
bottom, and the panel — plus the date line and the timeline slider, ~90px more
of height — into that spare column. `display:contents` on .sol-figwrap
dissolves the figure column so its children can be placed in either grid column
(the brand-bar technique). Measured: 973px square at 1920x1080 (was ~700), 793
at 1600x900, 613 at 1280x720. Portrait keeps the stacked layout, now scoped to
its own orientation query rather than inheriting the landscape placement — and
its column is minmax(0,1fr), because a bare 1fr took its floor from the
read-out rows' min-content width and pushed the stage 67px wider than the
phone.

**The zoom ladder and the layer toggles are gone from the planet pages.** Left
over from the last session: I removed the ladder but kept "Include: asteroid
belt / comets", which on a moon-system page can never do anything — the
small-bodies module is not even shipped there, so both buttons sat greyed out
under a heading about the solar system on a page about one planet. simCard now
takes a `layers` flag (does this page carry that module at all) and the whole
field is emitted only for the hub and the two pages that use it. A planet
page's panel ends with one link instead: the whole solar system. The jump-chip
row goes the same way — the breadcrumb above already offers Planets, and the
neighbours have their own card lower down.

Also fixed while in there: 29 links in the classroom lessons and two on the
orbital-velocity pages still pointed at /solar-system-simulator/<planet>/ and
/rocket-launches/, both redirects since those pages moved. All now come from
the registry.

### 2026-08-13 (3) — /planets/, and Saturn drawn the way Saturn looks

**A page whose subject is the planets.** There were eleven pages about
individual worlds and no page about "the planets" — the only way in was the
simulator, a tool, so a reader who wanted to know what Uranus is like had to
work out that the answer lived behind a zoom button. `/planets/` is the other
door: one card per world in orbital order, each a drawn globe, two paragraphs
of new prose (`overview` in solar-facts.json), five derived figures and a link
to that world's own page. The asteroid belt sits between Mars and Jupiter in
the same card shape — that is where it is — and Pluto is last with "Dwarf
planet" in its own kicker rather than being quietly promoted or quietly
dropped. New generator `build-planets.mjs`; the belt's picture is `solSvg` run
at build time, so the card cannot draw a belt in a different place from the
page it links to.

**It is a route, not a rival.** No moon tables, no open questions, no launch
windows: nothing that would put it in competition with its own children for
the same queries. Linked from the home card (now "The planets", pointing at
/planets/ rather than at the simulator), from the Space tab intro, from the
nav, from the simulator hub's "a page for every planet" card, and from every
planet page's breadcrumb.

**Breadcrumbs: Home / Planets / Jupiter**, under the H1, on every page in the
family. `solarCrumbs()` lives in solar-pages.mjs and the SAME array becomes the
BreadcrumbList JSON-LD, so the visible trail cannot disagree with the
structured one. The old trail said "Solar system simulator" in the middle,
which put a tool where the subject should be.

**The zoom ladder is the hub's control and nobody else's** (owner's call, and
right). On every other page it was six links into
`/solar-system-simulator/?zoom=…` sitting inside that page's own settings
panel — indistinguishable from a zoom control for the picture beside them, and
actually navigation to a different page with a different subject. Removed
there; the hub keeps them as buttons, where they genuinely repaint in place.
Same reasoning killed three more: the moon table's "watch this system move"
(it pointed into the hub at the moon rung, when that moving picture is at the
top of the same page), "go back to the whole system" (now names /planets/ and
the hub), and `data-sol-zoom2` on the closest-approach dates, which threw the
picture from Saturn's moons out to a heliocentric frame on a click that was
only supposed to change the date.

**Saturn.** Owner, with a Webb/Hubble reference image: the rings and the planet
do not look like the photographs. They didn't, and the cause was one number
doing two jobs. `glSvg` opened the rings by the AXIAL TILT — 26.7°, on every
day of a 29-year orbit — while projecting the bands as if the viewer sat on the
equator: two viewpoints in one picture, and a ring system that could never
close, even though the caption claimed it closes twice a Saturn year.
`glPt` now takes the sub-observer LATITUDE and the screen lean is one rotation
of the whole drawing; `glAspect()` solves the real sub-Earth latitude on the
ring plane from the IAU pole direction and the planet-to-Earth vector. Today
that is 9.0° (the crossing was 23 March 2025, which the solver reproduces to
the day), so the rings are drawn nearly edge-on, the way they actually look —
and Uranus, three quarters pole-on this decade, draws its rings as circles.
Each ring is a filled annulus now, not a stroked arc: a stroke is the same
width in every direction, which is what made a nearly edge-on system a smudge.
Added the rings' own shadow on the globe, its width set by the sub-SOLAR
latitude (also solved), and the apparent oblateness now depends on where you
are standing — pole-on, Saturn is a circle. Verified the eight non-ringed
planets render as before.

### 2026-08-13 (2) — Tilt on the moon views, and how many moons to draw

Four owner requests. **Tilt was dead on every moon view**: `satView` drew plain
circles and never read `opt.tilt`, so the slider moved and the picture did not —
on the pages where it matters most. Applied as a cosine on the vertical at each
computed y (not a wrapping squash), so orbits and rings flatten to ellipses
while moon discs and labels stay round and upright; one `satY()` keeps a moon on
its own ring. The read-out also claimed "orbit tilts ×N" on moon views where
nothing was exaggerated — now only claimed on heliocentric rungs.

**Default tilt 50** from a single `TILT_DEF` feeding the slider's baked value,
the client's `SOL_TILT0` and the build-time bake (which drew flat while the
slider said 45 — the only picture a crawler ever saw). **`?tilt=` round-trips**
and rides the share link.

**Moon detail levels.** L1 = the old picture, still default, so no existing link
changes. L2 = the regular system, L3 = the captured irregulars, each with its
own frame moon. `?moons=N` carries it; the caption states drawn vs confirmed;
hidden on Mars and Pluto, which already draw every moon they have. Drawn moons
35 → 89 (Saturn 9 → 30, including the ring shepherds).

**The data is checked, not believed.** All new moons pass the existing Kepler
gate untouched (worst 0.92% vs the 1.1% tolerance). That check caught two of my
own errors — a wrong Hippocamp period and an inconsistent Ananke a/period pair.
Setebos, Psamathe and Neso are deliberately omitted: no pair could be found that
agrees, and an inconsistent pair draws a moon sliding off its ring.

Also fixed: at L2+ the read-out ran out of the bottom of the card (nine rows fit
beside the picture, thirty do not) — capped, and scrolls.

published a/period pair turns up for them.

### 2026-08-13 — A page per simulator view, and a script per page

Every solar view but the planets was a `?zoom=` state on one URL: unlinkable,
unindexable, and 129KB of script on every page whether it drew moons or not.
New `seo/tools/solar-pages.mjs` is the single source of truth — URL, opening
rung, layers and JS modules per page — read by build-solar, build-sitemap and
the favicon rules, replacing three separate ideas of what pages exist.

URLs: the nine planets go flat (`/jupiter-and-moons-simulator/`,
`/earth-and-moon-simulator/`, and `/mercury-simulator/`/`/venus-simulator/` —
the "-and-moons" branch is decided by satellites.mjs's own moon list, so a URL
cannot promise moons a planet lacks). `/rocket-launches/` →
`/rocket-launch-simulator/` plus a child per destination. Two new heliocentric
views (inner-planets, outer-planets); belt and comets keep their URLs. Ten 301s
with splat forms; a pre-existing launch-windows redirect retargeted to avoid a
301→301 chain.

JS: one file per page from only the modules it draws with — 52KB (views), 58KB
(launch), 88–113KB (planets) against 129KB everywhere. What makes it possible is
the zoom ladder becoming links on every page but the hub, which keeps all
modules because climbing the ladder in place is its subject. Two checks make
omission safe rather than lucky: `assertNeeds()` refuses a page whose rung needs
a module it lacks (tested against four failure modes), and `SOL_HAS` blocks the
runtime route (`?belt=1`, layer buttons). `tilt` became a URL variable and rides
the share link. A destination page opening with `cfg.to` set exposed a real bug:
`DEST_SLUG` was var-hoisted but assigned after boot, so `markDest()` threw on
first load of the Mars page — both maps moved above the boot block.

No `/rocket-launch-simulator/moon/`: a lunar trip is an Earth-orbit departure,
not a heliocentric transfer, and transfer.mjs solves only the latter. The hub
says so in place of the link.

Unrelated but blocking: check-dates gated on four things dated 2026-08-12.
Solar-eclipse rolled to 2027-08-02 (the 6m23s Egypt totality, longest over land
until 2114) carrying 2026 forward per the carry-the-edition rule; Perseids to
2027 with the moon computed from the site's own solver (~80% gibbous, so the
copy says work around it, not "moonless").

Merged origin/main mid-flight (orbital-velocity simulator, view-ladder,
reordered solar panel, home moons cards) — 7 source conflicts, both sides kept;
main's new moons cards were repointed at planetPath() so they don't 301.

### 2026-08-09 — Per-page favicons
Every page shipped the same alarm-clock favicon; the hamburger menu already
draws a distinct glyph per tool, so the tab and the menu disagreed. `icons.mjs`
gains `faviconSvg(name)` (same `G` table, wrapped as a standalone `<svg
xmlns=…>`); `build-inline.mjs` writes one `/assets/favicon/<name>.svg` per icon
actually used and an ordered `FAVICON_RULES` prefix list (most specific first,
countdown-hub prefixes derived from `popular.categories` rather than hand-
listed) picks the icon per page inside `injectHeadTags`. Unmatched pages (home,
static/legal, anything new) keep `/favicon.svg`, the site's alarm-clock mark —
safe default, not a blank tab. The PNG/apple-touch-icon fallback stays one
raster site-wide (iOS bookmarks + Bing only, never the tab) — not worth a
render pass per glyph.
Two new glyphs, replacing weak spots in the existing `G` table (so the nav
menu improves too, not just the favicon): `globe` was abstract green blobs on
blue — redrawn as a recognizable Americas coastline (same hemisphere the
"Globe Showing Americas" emoji uses). `classroom` was a chalkboard whose chalk-
squiggle/desk-leg detail vanished at 16px — replaced with an apple, which holds
its two-lobe silhouette at favicon size. Verified all 15 favicons by rendering
to PNG via Playwright/Chromium at 16px/32px (temp dev dep, not committed) —
each reads as a distinct glyph, light and dark. Decided against a generic
"Space" icon: the three space-ish pages (Sun-Earth-Moon sim, Solar System sim,
Rocket Launches) already have distinct glyphs, and a shared one would undo the
point of per-page favicons.

### 2026-08-06 — The frame's height is fixed and its width is not
The owner's idea, and a better one than the fixed ratio it replaces: in full
screen the picture should take the container's WIDTH and let the height be
whatever is left, keeping one consistent set of proportions by sizing everything
against the height. It falls out neatly because of where the bodies are
anchored. ORR_H never changes, so every size in the drawing is fixed; the Earth
sits ORR_RIGHT in from the RIGHT edge and the sun ORR_SX in from the LEFT, so a
wider frame does exactly one thing — put more sky between them.

That is the right thing for extra width to do. The sun's distance is by far the
most wrong figure in the picture, so every pixel of it makes the picture less
wrong: 3,151x too close on the card, 1,563x on a phone held sideways, 1,057x at
the 1,100-unit cap. Nothing about the bodies moves, so it is recognisably the
same drawing at any width, and the scale card now says so rather than quoting
one figure as if it were the only one.

orrSvg() takes an optional frame width; the client measures its own box and
passes the ratio, on boot, on fullscreenchange and on a debounced resize. 480 is
the floor and the card's default, so the card and the /sun/ and /moon/ figures
are untouched. Measured in Chromium: 100% width fill at iPhone SE landscape
(798x270), iPhone 14 (961x270), iPad (480x270 — that box is taller than the
default, so the default is already right) and desktop (592x270).

### 2026-08-06 — Sun centred left, everything smaller, phase in full screen
THE SUN MOVED TO THE MIDDLE OF THE LEFT EDGE, cut off on one side instead of
two. In the corner it left the whole bottom-left of the frame empty and put the
sun-Earth line at a 12-degree tilt; centred, it uses the left side's full height
and that line is exactly horizontal, so the terminator is exactly vertical.

EVERYTHING GOT SMALLER, which is the counter-intuitive part and the right call.
Filling the frame with three big discs is the LEAST realistic arrangement
available: what is most wrong here is distance, distance is measured in
Earth-diameters, so shrinking the bodies while keeping the orbit wide improves
every figure on the scale card at once. Earth 46 -> 42, sun 93 -> 88, moon
12.5 -> 11.45, orbit 108 -> 112. Result: moon 26x -> 23x too close, sun 3,905x
-> 3,151x, sun size 54x -> 52x too small, and the clear sky between the sun's
disc and the moon's from 63 units to 101. The floor is legibility — the moon
still has to show a lit half and a dark half on a phone, which is ~19px at
390px wide, and that is what stopped the shrinking there rather than further.

THE PHASE IS IN FULL SCREEN NOW, beside the instant read-out. It went through
two other placements first. A row of its own directly above the slider cost the
picture about 15% of its width, because a 16:9 frame in a wider box is limited
by its short side, so every row beneath it eats width. Sharing the BUTTON row
was worse: the buttons are left-justified and wide, and reserving a centre
column for the phase wrapped them onto two lines. The read-out row was already
centred and already there, so the phase lands in the middle of the screen for
nothing.

### 2026-08-06 — The orrery goes landscape, and the simulator gets a full screen
THE FRAME IS 16:9, 480x270, everywhere the orrery appears — /sun/, /moon/ and
all 1,104 simulator pages. It was square on the reasoning that this is a view of
a plane; true, but the plane has a long axis, and the square frame spent its
height on nothing. At 440px wide the landscape figure is still SHORTER than the
old 340px square, so the cards did not grow while the picture got bigger.

THE SUN IS ON THE FRAME RATHER THAN IN THE CORNER, and the two constraints pull
against each other: it has to run off the left and top edges while leaving the
corner itself clear. Pushing the centre out along the diagonal grows the corner
and shrinks the overhang. 75/75 with r=93 gives 18 units of overhang on each
edge and a corner sliver whose arc meets both edges 20 units out — both
comfortably visible. The sun's disc is now BIGGER than the Earth's (93 to 46),
which it never was before, and at new moon the moon still clears it by 63 units.

THE SUN'S PINNED ANGLE IS DERIVED, not typed. Moving it off the corner diagonal
broke the fixed 45-degree day/night line, so the terminator is now the sun's
screen angle turned a quarter turn either way, and that angle comes from
ORR_SX/ORR_SY. The lit half cannot stop facing the sun.

THE CARD IS THE FULL-SCREEN VIEW. Play / Now / Settings / Full screen,
left-justified over a full-width slider, wrapped in one .sim-stage — and that
stage is what goes full screen, so there is one layout rather than two. Where,
when and how long the slider covers moved into a <dialog> that applies on SAVE;
the city search went with them, and the header is now just the place name and
its coordinates, linked to the spot on a map. The time zone that used to sit
there is gone.

TWO THINGS THE BROWSER MADE HARDER THAN EXPECTED, both caught by driving the
real page in Chromium rather than reasoning about it. `sim-set` was already the
SUNSET read-out's id, so the new Settings button rendered as "8:37 PM" until it
was renamed. And the fullscreen element cannot be the one that rotates: the UA
stylesheet applies transform:none !important to it, so the portrait fallback
turns an inner wrapper instead — with flex-shrink off, or the deliberately
over-wide block gets squeezed back before the rotation lands.

Scale figures moved on their own, as designed: moon 26x too close, sun 3,905x
and 54x too small (the sun being properly large is most of that). The marble
walk is unchanged, because it describes the real system and not the drawing.

### 2026-08-06 — The home card's moon changes phase
The moon in the orbit stays half lit, because from above it always is. The PHASE
is a second disc in the corner — the same moon seen from the ground, which is
the pairing the simulator page makes too — and it is synchronised to the orbit
beside it rather than merely looping at the same rate: HM_DELAY works out from
the drawn positions of sun, Earth and moon how far round the orbit full moon
falls, and offsets the phase animation by exactly that. Stepped through a full
cycle in Chromium to check: full when the moon is opposite the sun, new when it
passes it, and the crescent on the correct side either way.

Three CSS animations draw it, which is what a real phase costs: the terminator
is an ELLIPSE whose width runs full-nothing-full twice a cycle (the shape a
day/night line actually projects to), its fill flips at the quarters so the
ellipse subtracts before first quarter and adds after, and the disc mirrors at
full moon because waxing and waning are lit on opposite sides. All off under
prefers-reduced-motion, with the terminator pinned to first quarter so what is
left is unambiguously a moon.

### 2026-08-06 — Full-site review pass: the escape bug, the gates, and the caching
Worked the 2026-08-06 review end to end. The headline was a DOUBLE-ESCAPED
TITLE on 1,103 simulator city pages ("Sun, Moon &amp;amp; Earth") reaching the
tab, the SERP, the JSON-LD name and the og-image text. Fixed at the one line
that passed a pre-escaped string into head() — and then check-pages.mjs grew a
gate for the whole class: every ld+json block must parse, and no double-escaped
entity may appear in a title, description, og:* tag or any JSON-LD string. The
gate immediately found twelve more pages the review had missed — the entire
/solar-system-simulator/ family had the same bug.

check-planets.mjs printed FAIL and exited 0, which made the proof of the
element table something only a human reading the output could believe. It now
accumulates a flag and exits non-zero (verified by flipping a threshold), and
`npm run check` runs it.

CACHING: the safe half only, by decision. Dropped must-revalidate and added
stale-while-revalidate=300, so a repeat visitor paints from cache and
revalidates behind the paint — the largest repeat-visit win available on a site
whose HTML *is* the render-critical resource. s-maxage STAYS at 300: raising it
to 3600 only pays once a deploy purges the zone cache, and that needs CF_ZONE_ID
+ a purge token in the repo secrets. Both _headers and rebuild.yml now say so.

THE SOLAR SCALE PROSE WAS OUT BY TWO, in opposite directions: orbitPx returned a
radius while every sentence said "across", and bodyPx carried a spare *2. On the
page whose identity is "the copy and the picture cannot disagree" the copy
disagreed with the picture. Both fixed at the definition; the classroom
sentence that quoted it now IMPORTS the figure rather than typing it. The "why
the frame is square" paragraph was a ternary returning "40" either way — now
computed against a 16:9 frame of the same WIDTH (87%), which is the constraint
that actually binds.

Also: the solar page baked its hero from the same solSvg the browser runs (it
shipped an empty div); every simulator control now ships disabled and is handed
over at boot; replaceState is debounced and skipped during Play (Safari's
100-per-30s limit was exhausted four seconds in); the date picker stops at 2050
where the elements do; orreryNote stopped dropping its `live` argument, so
1,103 baked captions no longer say "right now" about a build-time instant.

DIFFERENTIATION over duplication: teachCard and classroomCard moved to the hub
(city pages 92.5KB -> 82.9KB), the identical FAQ became four computed per-city
questions, and each city gained its own moon-sun angle and moonrise-drift rows.
The family also joined check-crosslinks, which now asserts 6,882 reciprocal
links across 3,606 pages.
