### 2026-08-06 — The review's smaller half: images, identity, feeds, icons
B1's premise did not survive measurement. The seven event heroes were called
"near-lossless at 0.2 B/px"; they are dense pen-and-ink illustrations, which is
the content lossy compression is worst at — WebP q75 saves 7%, q50 smears the
linework, lossless is 457KB. What DOES work is AVIF (same picture, ~70% of the
bytes) and a 384w variant for the 340px box the image actually renders in.
make-event-images.mjs writes both; build-events probes for them on disk, so a
missing variant degrades to the plain <img> it always had.

B2 was the real defect underneath it: every hero was emitted as 704x536 while
the files are 700x546, 700x499, 704x532, 704x543 and 700x540 — a wrong reserved
box and a visible shift on decode. Dimensions are now READ FROM THE FILE by a
ten-line WebP header parse, no dependency.

IDENTITY: og:site_name was on zero pages and no Organization node existed
anywhere. Both injected once, in build-inline and webSiteLd. The logo needed a
raster (Google does not reliably index SVG for this, and Bing's favicon
pipeline and iOS both want PNG), so make-logo-raster.mjs renders 512 and 180
from the same favicon.svg the pages use.

New: /calendar/events.ics (a VCALENDAR of all 182 dates, all-day, stable UIDs,
RFC 5545 folding) and /llms.txt, whose family counts are DERIVED from the
sitemap rows so it cannot claim pages the site does not have. LearningResource
on the two simulator hubs and /classroom/ only — realistic about it: no rich
result exists, the value is AI answer engines and OER aggregators, and putting
it on 1,103 city pages would repeat the duplication the FAQs just undid.

The menu ran THREE IDENTICAL GLOBES (World Clock, Sun-Earth-Moon, Solar System)
and reused the timer glyph for the teachers' guide. Three new icons in the same
hand-drawn style: an Earth with a lit moon beside it, a sun with two orbit rings
and two planets, and a chalkboard.

Popular and trending both fell back to the same complete date-ordered list, so
without view counts they were two indexable pages differing by an H1. They now
fall back to different things, each honest for its own question: /popular/ to
the curated editorial picks, /trending/ to the next 30 days.

TOUR DE FRANCE, finally carried forward. Pogacar's fifth, level with Anquetil,
Merckx, Hinault and Indurain; 6:26 over Evenepoel, del Toro third and in white,
Pedersen green, Carapaz polka dots, Barcelona to Paris. Corroborated across Al
Jazeera, NBC Sports, Cyclingnews and two cycling outlets before it went in.
check-volatile's carry-forward finding is clear.

### 2026-08-05 — Both simulators on the home page, the menu, and /classroom/
Three cards on the home page — Sun, Earth & Moon; Solar System; For teachers —
and all three entries in the hamburger menu, which now runs to eleven: seven
tools, two simulators, the teachers' guide and Countdowns.

STATIC, AS ASKED, AND MEASURED. Both simulators are heavy pages (a solver, a
drawing loop, a slider) and none of that ships to the home page: each card is
one baked SVG and a link. The first attempt used the REAL solar drawing for the
thumbnail — 5.6KB, and at 128px a 960px-wide frame turns the planets into
sub-pixel mush, so it was replaced with a schematic drawn at the size it is
actually seen, at a tenth of the bytes. Three cards for +4KB on a 204KB page.

/classroom/ is no longer only about the timer and the stopwatch: it is
"Classroom Tools for Teachers", with a Science card carrying both simulators and
the specific lessons each one supports — day and night, the phases, the seasons,
the midnight sun, tidal locking; then how empty space is, why the solar system
will not fit on a screen, and how differently the planets move. Plus the
corridor walk, and an FAQ entry on running them on a projector.

### 2026-08-05 — /solar-system-simulator/
The planets, on their real orbits, with the same control pattern as the other
simulator. Two new files: `planets.mjs` (Keplerian elements + rates, Kepler by
Newton–Raphson, ~arcminutes over 1800–2050) and `build-solar.mjs`.

THE ELEMENT TABLE IS PROVEN, NOT TRUSTED — `check-planets.mjs`, because a wall
of digits with one wrong produces a plausible picture that is wrong. Earth's
heliocentric longitude against the SUN's geocentric longitude out of moon.mjs (a
different series, written years earlier): they agree to 0.16°. Every derived
period against the known one: within 0.08%. Mercury's and Venus's greatest
elongations swept from the geometry: 17.9–27.8° and 45.4–47.0°, against the
textbook 18–28 and 45–47 — which validates both inner orbits AND Earth's,
eccentricities and orientations included.

THE ZOOM LADDER IS THE ANSWER TO SCALE, and the numbers decided it, not taste.
The outer:inner orbit ratio is what governs legibility: 4:1 out to Mars, 25:1 to
Saturn, 78:1 to Neptune — where Mercury's entire orbit is 3px and Earth's 10px.
So there are four rungs, and the outermost keeps the inner four as a labelled
knot on purpose: that IS the shape of the solar system, and it is exactly what
evenly-spaced textbook diagrams hide.

THE EARTH & MOON RUNG IS THE ONE TO LOOK AT. It is the only view on the site to
scale in size and distance at once, and the first attempt got it wrong — a 22px
Earth put the moon 1,290px off-frame. Fixed by letting the physics set the
scale: the moon's mean orbit fills the frame, so Earth comes out about 7px
across and the moon under 2, with thin locator rings so they can be found at
all. That is not a drawing choice; it is what 30 Earth-diameters of empty space
does to a picture, and the figure says so in its own caption.

No other moons, and the page says why rather than apologising: at the zoom where
Jupiter is visible, its outermost Galilean is under half a pixel away, and this
module does not solve for their positions. Drawing them would be two untruths
for a busier picture. Spans are month/year/decade/century because Jupiter moves
2° in a month and Neptune 0°; a century still is not one Neptune lap (165 years),
which the page turns into a fact worth knowing.

### 2026-08-05 — The classroom card, rewritten to match the page it describes
It was five bullets written before the page had a Play button, phase-jump
buttons, a labelled phase disc, a moon–sun angle row, a link builder or 1,103
city pages, and it had drifted into describing controls that were no longer the
ones on screen. Rewritten around what is actually there, in three groups: the
two big questions to start with (day and night; why the moon has phases, read
off the moon–sun angle and the phase disc together), seven follow-ons that each
name a specific control and a specific row — the full-moon jump then a day span
to find its rise time, moonrise sliding ~50 minutes a day, two solstice links
for the same city, the poles, two cities at one instant built with the builder,
and spring/neap tides read off the angle alone — and one away-from-the-screen
activity, the corridor walk from the scale card.

The "two views of one moon" item is new and is there because it is the question
the picture provokes: the moon in the drawing looks half lit at every phase, the
disc beside the read-out is the same moon from the ground, and "which one is what
it really looks like" is a good twenty minutes with a class. The card also now
says the page is free, sign-up-free and entirely client-side, and that a link
made today still works next term — the two things a teacher needs to know before
planning around it.

### 2026-08-05 — The link builder becomes a form, and two contrast fixes
"Make a link to a particular sky" was a readonly mirror of the address bar. It
is a form now: place (a select seeded with the 128 curated cities and refilled
with all 1,103 the moment it is opened), starting date, starting time, and how
much the slider covers. The URL rebuilds on every change and sits at the BOTTOM
of the form, and under it a sentence says in words where the link goes —
"Opens the simulator for **Seattle**, starting **Wednesday, August 5, 2026** at
**3:04 PM** local time, with the slider covering **30 days**. At that moment the
moon there is a **last quarter**, **52%** lit, and the sun is **52.1°** above the
horizon." Every figure the URL encodes is repeated in readable form, plus what
the sky actually does then, which is the part that catches a link built for the
wrong month or the wrong hemisphere before it goes to thirty people.

The form follows the view until someone edits a field, then it is theirs;
"Match the view above" hands it back. Verified end to end: seeded from the view,
independent after an edit, re-synced by the chip, and the URL it produced opened
exactly the sky it described. The parameter table moved into a <details>.

TWO CONTRAST FIXES. The reader's daily circle used to be drawn UNDER the night
shading, which dimmed the half they most want to follow — the night half, where
their own spot is when the question is "am I in the dark yet". Its near half is
now drawn over the shading at one strength the whole way round (same colour; the
day/night line it crosses already says which part is which), while the far half
stays underneath, because that part really is behind the globe. The moon's orbit
ring went from .22 to .34 opacity with a tighter dash — present, still clearly
a schematic.

### 2026-08-05 — One "also see" line, on all three families
The simulator pointer was a bare link. It is now a sentence that names the other
two families for THE SAME PLACE first and the simulator second: a sun page reads
"Also see moonrise, moonset & the phase in Seattle, and the Sun, Earth & Moon
movement simulator for Seattle, to watch how the three move together over a day,
a week or a month"; a moon page swaps sun for moon; a tide page names both. The
family rendering it is the link left out, so nothing points at itself.

Placement follows where each page already talks about the others: on sun and
moon pages it sits under the picture, after the tide sentence; on tide pages it
is inside the cross-link strip, directly beneath the sun and moon tiles.

Audited rather than assumed: all 2,206 sun and moon city pages link to their OWN
city's simulator page and every target exists. 35 tide stations sit more than 35
miles from any city we publish, so they have no paired place and previously got
no strip at all; they now get a short card of their own with the hub link, since
the simulator works from any coordinates even where local sun and moon pages do
not exist. 66 + 35 = every station page.

### 2026-08-05 — A simulator page for every city, and the teaching card
1,103 city pages under /sun-moon-earth-movement-simulator/<slug>/ — exact parity
with /sun/ and /moon/, from the same registry, chosen over a tighter set because
parity is an invariant the build already enforces. The dilution risk of 1,103
near-duplicates was raised and accepted; the answer to it is that each page
carries things only that city has: its own picture baked for the build minute
(so a crawler and a no-JS visitor get a real sky), today's sunrise, sunset and
day length, tonight's moon and its rise/set, the longest and shortest day of its
year, and a sentence generated from its LATITUDE BAND — inside the polar circle,
above 45°, mid, or tropics — each of which says something different and true.
The controller is hoisted now (~60KB, byte-identical across 1,104 pages) with
only the config line inline, which is the opposite of the right call for a
single page and the right one for a family.

Reachability was the other design point: each city's /sun/ and /moon/ page links
straight to ITS simulator page rather than the hub, so every child has two
strong inbound links from pages that already rank; the hub lists the 120 largest
rather than pretending to list 1,103. Tide pages link to their paired city's.

THE TEACHING CARD, on all 1,104: the moon keeps one face turned to us (tidal
locking, 27.3 days for both spin and orbit, libration showing us 59% over time)
and therefore "the dark side of the moon" is a misnomer — at new moon the far
side is the fully lit one; why moonrise slips ~50 minutes a day (13° of orbit
per rotation); sidereal vs synodic month and why they differ by two days; the
phase being identical worldwide but rotated by hemisphere; why a 5.1° orbital
tilt means no monthly eclipse — which is also why this flat view refuses to draw
one; and spring/neap tides read off the moon-sun angle. Every number is a named
constant next to the other real-world figures rather than loose in prose.

One gate catch worth recording: a bare `.card h3` rule in the orrery CSS part
made sectionsNeeded() claim every methodology page needed the orrery section.
The build said so and refused; the rule is scoped to `.sim-teach h3` instead.

### 2026-08-05 — Simulator: the month is the default, and the phase disc says which view it is
Two follow-ups. The span now defaults to a MONTH, not a day: a day shows the
Earth turning, which the card on every /sun/ and /moon/ page already shows, so
landing on a day made the new page look like the old card. The month is the
thing a day cannot contain — the moon going right round, phase and all — and now
the first drag teaches it. Jumping to a phase keeps whatever span is set instead
of forcing a day, for the same reason.

The phase disc gained a line saying what it is: "from the ground at Aug 28,
4:18 AM". The moon in the DRAWING is half lit at every phase because that view
looks down on it and sees the terminator edge-on — which is exactly the lesson
(the phase is the ANGLE, not the lit fraction) — but two views of one object in
one card have to be labelled or they read as a contradiction. Considered and
rejected: drawing the Earth-view phase onto the moon in the diagram. It would
mix two viewpoints in one picture, and the picture's whole job is to show that
the phase falls out of the angle.

### 2026-08-05 — /sun-moon-earth-movement-simulator/
The card's slider can only ever scrub one day, because the page it sits on is
about one day — and a week is where the interesting things start. So the week
and the month got a page of their own: the same picture at 520px, a day / week /
month span control, a slider across whatever span is chosen, Play (a full sweep
in ~25s, which is the only way a month is readable — nobody drags that slowly),
jump-to-next-phase buttons, a read-out of both bodies' altitude and direction,
the moon-sun angle, sunrise/sunset, moonrise/moonset and day length, and a large
phase disc rendered by the site's own moon face.

THE SCALE CARD IS COMPUTED, NOT WRITTEN. orrery.mjs now exports ORR_GEOM — the
drawing's own dimensions — and the generator divides those into the real ones:
the moon comes out 27x too close, the sun 5,144x too close and 114x too small,
and the moon's SIZE against Earth is very nearly right (25% drawn vs 27% real).
The corridor version is the part a class remembers: shrink the Earth to a 16 mm
marble and the moon is a 4.4 mm bead 48 cm away, while the sun is a 1.7 m ball
188 m down the road. Change the picture and every one of those numbers changes
with it — there is no sentence on that card that can quietly become false.

Everything is in the URL — `city` or `lat`+`lon`+`tz`+`name`, `date`, `time`,
`span` — so a teacher hands out one link and thirty screens show the same sky.
The address bar is rewritten as the controls move, and a builder near the
bottom copies it. The three families point at the page from beside the picture
it enlarges (sun and moon under the view, tides in the cross-link strip, since
tide pages have no view of their own); the page points back at whichever place
is loaded, including its tide station and world-clock page where they exist.

Not hoisted into a shared JS file, deliberately: hoisting pays for itself across
a thousand identical pages, and this is one page, where it would only buy a
second round trip.

### 2026-08-05 — The view becomes a time machine (date, time and a day slider)
The picture solved for an arbitrary instant from the first commit; it just never
let anyone move it. Now the stamp under it is a control: a native
`datetime-local` field holding the PLACE's wall clock, a slider across the whole
day, and Now.

THE SPLIT BETWEEN THE TWO CONTROLS IS THE DESIGN. The field changes the DAY and
the whole page follows — sunrise, dial, twilight bands, 7-day table, the answer
sentence, and on /moon/ the hero, tables and 30-day strip — because per-day
facts are what those cards are, and a page showing two different days at once is
exactly the confusion a date control is supposed to remove. The slider changes
only the TIME, and moves nothing but this card: the day's facts have not
changed, only where the two bodies are in it, so a drag costs one solar
position, one lunar one and a string of SVG. It repaints on `input`, so the
picture follows the thumb. The page's own date pickers push back the other way,
keeping the time of day, so the two controls cannot disagree.

WHEN IT IS NOT NOW, EVERYTHING SAYS SO. Heading ("Where the sun **will be**" /
"**was**"), the phase row's label ("At that time"), the moon line, the phase note
and the caption ("at the time shown", "then") all switch. That was not polish:
the card is titled "right now", and a picture of next October under numbers
still labelled "right now" would be a lie the control itself created.

Time zones are the fiddly part and are handled once, in orrery.mjs: a
datetime-local value is a naive wall-clock reading with no zone, which is
exactly right here — it is the place's clock, never the visitor's — and
`orrParse` resolves it through the zone in two passes so a DST change cannot
land it an hour out. A local time that does not exist (the spring-forward gap)
comes back as a real instant to one side of it, because there is no such time
and so no sky to draw.

Both controls ship inert — the field `disabled`, Now `hidden` — and the painter
enables them, because without JS neither could do anything and a dead control is
worse than none. The row is one line at 340px (the label is the first thing to
drop on a narrow phone, then it wraps rather than clipping Now).

### 2026-08-05 — The view leads the card; tomorrow's sunrise is a real link
Two changes after seeing it on a phone.

(1) THE HORIZON PLOT IS GONE and the Sun–Earth–Moon view has its place at the
top of "Where the sun is right now", with the live stat rows under it. The old
plot marked the sun and moon against an E–S–W line — which is precisely what
the Direction and Azimuth rows already state, in words, six inches below it.
Removing it took the build-time `skyFigure()`, its client twin `skyFig()` and
the four raw altitude/azimuth fields that existed only to feed them. The card
now reads picture → numbers → explanation, so `orreryFigure()` and
`orreryCaption()` are separate exports: the sun card puts its stats between
them, the moon card runs them together. `aria-describedby` on the figure keeps
the two associated, which matters because the SVG is aria-hidden. The stat grid
went full-width and needed a new breakpoint — one column below 560px, two
above; at the old 420px it left two cramped columns with "Full daylight"
wrapping.

(2) "TOMORROW'S SUNRISE" NOW LOADS TOMORROW. It was an in-page anchor to the
tomorrow card; it is now `/sun/<city>/?date=<tomorrow>`, and the page reads
`?date=YYYY-MM-DD` on load and renders everything for that day through the date
picker it already had. The href is baked from the build's tomorrow and re-set by
paintTomorrow() from the VISITOR's tomorrow, which at 11pm local is already a
different date. A bad or out-of-range param falls through to today.
The answer-first sentence had to change with it: on any date but today it is
rewritten to "On Thursday, August 6, 2026 the sun rises in Seattle at…", because
a page reached by the tomorrow link, showing tomorrow in every card, must not
open with a line that says "today". Returning to today restores the baked
markup so the live puts keep working.

ON THE SEO EXPECTATION, stated plainly: this does not create a second ranking
URL. The page is static, so a crawler at `?date=…` gets byte-identical HTML with
a self-referential canonical pointing at the clean URL — which is the correct
setup (the dated variants consolidate into the real page rather than competing
with it), but it means the win here is the reader's, not the ranking's. What
actually targets "sunrise tomorrow in X" is the existing tomorrow card's own
content and H2. A genuinely indexable variant would have to be a separate static
page per city with its own differentiated copy — ~1,074 more pages — and that is
a decision, not a side effect of a link.

### 2026-08-05 — Orrery: a drawn-at stamp and an Update button
The picture is repainted on load and every 30–60 seconds, but nothing on screen
said which instant it was of — so a tab left open all afternoon showed a sky
from lunchtime with no way to tell. There is now a line under it: "Drawn for
Aug 5, 1:53:56 PM", in the PLACE's clock like every other time on the page, and
an Update button beside it that redraws for the moment it is pressed. Seconds
are shown deliberately: without them Update looks like it did nothing.
`orrPaint` remembers its last arguments, so the button needs no wiring from
either generator — and on /moon/near-me/, where the place changes underneath it,
it redraws the place the visitor has now. It ships `hidden` and is revealed by
the painter, because with no JS it could not do anything.

### 2026-08-05 — The view on /moon/ too, and what the angle does to the tides
Two follow-ups to the entry below. (1) `/moon/<city>/` now carries the same
picture, in its own "Where the moon is right now" card above the 30-day strip.
It is a separate card and not another row in the hero for one reason: the hero
moves with the date picker and this does not — it is painted from `Date.now()`
and has its own minute tick, so "right now" still means right now while the
visitor is reading some other date. `/moon/near-me/` gets it too, labelled with
whatever place the visitor resolved (including the reverse-geocoded upgrade).
(2) The angle the picture shows is not only the phase — it is what makes tides
big or small — so `crosslinks.tideNote()` says that in two sentences under the
figure and links to the reader's own station.

Three things this needed structurally. The markup, the painter and the CSS are
now ONE component (`orreryFigure()` / `orrPaint()` / `20d2-orrery.css`), because
the alternative was a moon page reusing `.sun-*` classes — which the `sun` probe
would have matched, inlining the whole sun stylesheet (dial, year chart, golden
hour) into 1,103 moon pages for one figure. The new `orrery` section is ~350
bytes and both families get exactly it. The tide link resolves through
`cityRef(slug).tide`, not the reciprocal pair map the cross-link strip uses: the
pair map says Los Angeles has no tides, because its station sits at San Pedro
and chose a nearer city. That is the right answer for a structural two-way link
and the wrong one for "where are your tide times", so this anchor carries no
`data-xlink` and check-crosslinks correctly ignores it; check-pages still proves
it resolves. Inland and non-US places get the explanation and no link — naming a
station 900 miles away would be worse than saying nothing.

The tide sentence claims no more than it can: the mechanism (moon does most of
the pulling, sun about half as much, in line = spring, square = neap), the lag
("the coast follows a day or two behind"), and that the shore's shape matters as
much as the sky — then hands over to the real predictions. No statement about
today's range, which is not something a phase angle can tell you.
Verified in a browser on both families: the marker the page draws matches what
Node draws for the same instant, with the clock shifted 7 hours to prove the
live path actually runs.

### 2026-08-05 — The Sun–Earth–Moon view on /sun/ place pages
"Where the sun is right now" said how high and which way; it could not say WHY.
New `seo/tools/orrery.mjs` draws the same instant from outside: the sun pinned
to the top-left corner, its two edge rays landing exactly on the ends of the
day/night line, the reader's own city as a marker on the globe, and the moon at
its true direction around it. Rendered into every `/sun/<city>/` page at build
time and repainted live with the rest of the card (~1,074 pages, plus
`/sun/anywhere/` and `/sun/near-me/`, where the marker takes the resolved
place name).

The viewpoint is above the Earth's ORBIT, looking down from the reader's own
hemisphere. That choice is the whole design: it is the one vantage where the
moon's separation from the sun — which IS the phase — is not foreshortened, and
flipping it below the equator keeps southern cities from spending their day
hidden behind the globe. Verified numerically, not by eye: the lit half always
matches the sun altitude the card prints, the drawn moon angle matches the
elongation the caption quotes, the pole leans sunward in June and away in
December, and the fraction of the reader's daily circle on the lit side lands
within 1–4 points of the day length the page prints (the gap is refraction —
the real sunrise is at −0.833°, the circle splits at 0°).

What it deliberately does NOT draw: Earth's shadow. The real umbra reaches well
past the moon's orbit, so a geometric cone would put the moon in it every full
moon and imply a monthly eclipse — and whether one happens turns on the moon's
distance from the ecliptic, the one thing this projection flattens away. The
moon's orbit radius is schematic and the caption says so rather than inviting a
reader to measure a picture that cannot be measured.

One source, two runtimes (the moon.mjs pattern): the drawing is one ES5 string,
inlined for the browser and instantiated through `new Function` for the build,
so there is no Node twin to drift. It reads the sun and moon positions from
MOON_CORE, already on the page — the picture and the numbers beside it cannot
disagree. Files: `seo/tools/orrery.mjs` (new), `build-sun.mjs`,
`assets/css/parts/20d-sun2.css`. Adds ~3.4KB of SVG per page and ~2KB to the
shared, hashed sun-city controller.

Open: `/moon/<city>/` pages have the same card shape and would carry this well,
but that is a second family and this session did one feature. (Done in the
entry above, together with the tide sentence.)

### 2026-08-01 — IndexNow: removals announced, per-URL revisions, state only on success
FIRST, A CORRECTION. The previous entry claimed build-sitemap now derives each
family's source set from the generator's imports. It does not — or did not: the
end-to-end test I ran did `git reset --hard HEAD~1` to undo a temporary commit,
which also discarded the uncommitted deps()/srcOf work. The rebuild and commit
that followed contained only regenerated HTML; build-sitemap.mjs wasn't in the
commit at all. The simulation quoted in that entry was real arithmetic but ran
in a standalone script, so it never proved the file had changed. Re-applied here
and verified by grepping the committed file, not a copy of the logic.
Then the three changes asked for:
(1) REMOVED URLS ARE SUBMITTED. IndexNow covers added, updated and deleted; a
retired page was previously pruned from the state silently, so the engine only
learned about it whenever it next crawled. A URL in the state but not in the
sitemap now goes into the payload — removals FIRST, since a dead URL in an index
is worse than a stale live one — and is deleted from the state only once that
payload was accepted. A removal capped out of a batch stays in the file so the
next run still announces it.
(2) PER-URL REVISIONS instead of date-only. build-sitemap writes
seo/_data/sitemap-revs.json (`url -> "<lastmod>/<rev>"`), rev being a hash of
the content of the generator and every module it imports. The public sitemap is
untouched and still date-only, which is what the spec wants. This closes a real
miss: two edits on the same day produced an identical sitemap AND an identical
state entry, so the second was never submitted — and on this repo two edits a
day is normal. Proved: a second same-day edit to localtime.mjs now queues 2,546
URLs where date-only saw nothing. The workflow's path filter had to learn about
the sidecar too, since that same-day case leaves sitemap.xml byte-identical.
(3) STATE ONLY AFTER 200/202 — already true for updates, now also true for
removals, and scoped to the URLs actually in the payload.
MIGRATION: legacy date-only entries are treated as STALE, so the next run
re-announces the site once (3,008 URLs) and is exact from then on. I first wrote
it to match on the date half to avoid that bulk run, and testing showed why that
was wrong: a legacy entry only upgrades when submitted, and under date-matching
a same-day edit never looks like a submission — the entries would have sat in
the old format indefinitely, blind to exactly the case this change exists for.
Files: build-sitemap.mjs, indexnow.mjs, indexnow.yml, CLAUDE.md. Verified: full
build + all three gates; legacy state → one clean bulk run; post-migration state
→ no-op; same-day change → detected; removed URL → announced then dropped.
NOTE FOR NEXT TIME: never run build-sitemap.mjs alone. It imports build-sun,
build-moon and build-world-clock, whose top level WRITES their pages — without
build-inline afterwards those 109 world-clock pages lose their inlined CSS. Run
`npm run build`.

### 2026-07-31 — Sitemap lastmod: derive the source set instead of hand-listing it
Found while auditing whether IndexNow was actually working (it is — six clean
runs, exact volumes, state in sync with the sitemap). The gap was upstream of it:
each family's `<lastmod>` came from a HAND-WRITTEN list of source files, and
those lists named only the generator and its data. build-sun.mjs imports
localtime.mjs and crosslinks.mjs, which render a line and a strip on ~2,330 sun,
moon and tide pages — so a change confined to one of those modules moved no
lastmod, and IndexNow announced nothing. Invisible failure: the sitemap stayed
valid, it was just quietly wrong about what was new. It nearly bit us for real —
the local-time line shipped at 01:44, its run failed on key verification, and
those pages only got announced because the 02:37 manual run happened to submit
everything.
`deps()` now walks each generator's own imports transitively (static `from
"./x"` and dynamic `import("./x")`, since several generators use the latter) and
the per-family lists are derived from it. Data files aren't imports, so they stay
named explicitly. Proved with a simulation: hold build-sun.mjs at its
pre-today date and the old list reports the sun pages unchanged since
2026-07-27, while the derived set correctly reports 2026-07-31, driven by
localtime.mjs / city-registry.mjs / wc-cities.mjs.
KNOWN TRADE: lib.mjs is imported by nearly everything, so a change there now
bumps every family and re-announces most of the site. That is defensible —
lib.mjs holds brand(), the GA snippet and the JSON-LD helpers, so a change
there really does change every page — but it is worth knowing before editing it
casually. Zero churn from this change itself: no URL's lastmod moved.
Files: build-sitemap.mjs, CLAUDE.md. Verified: build + all three gates pass.

### 2026-07-31 — /classroom/, the teacher's guide
The link-worthy asset from the SEO review, built. /timer/ and /stopwatch/ answer
"I need a timer now"; this answers "how do I run this in a room of thirty
children, on a projector, without it letting me down mid-lesson" — a different
search, and the thing a teacher-resource site can actually link to (nobody links
to a tool, they link to an explanation of how to use one).
Nine sections: one-tap presets (7 lengths, each an existing page, with what each
is for), projector/full-screen, the keyboard shortcuts, transitions and
three-at-once rotations, reading fluency and repeated trials with laps, SHARING
THE MEASUREMENTS (owner's addition — CSV for records, Copy for a doc, the
session image for a person, and the fact that nothing is uploaded), what a
browser timer can't do, accessibility, and 6 FAQs with FAQPage JSON-LD.
EVERY CLAIM VERIFIED against the emitted pages first, not assumed: Space/R on a
duration page, Space/L/R on the stopwatch, no shortcuts on the multi-timer hub
(stated as such), three timers, CSV + Copy + Share image, both full-screen
modes. A teacher who plans a lesson around a feature that doesn't exist is let
down in front of a class. The accessibility section says the alarm is sound-only
and that the display is deliberately not announced every tick — real limits, not
a compliance paragraph.
Inbound links added from the timer hub and the stopwatch (which also got a
descriptive "online stopwatch with lap times" anchor in place of a bare link,
per the review). Also fixed a genuine CSS gap: prose bullets inside a card had
no class, and `.cd-meta` — the only list class — is a flex key/value layout that
put every inline <em> on its own line. Added `.bullets`. Files:
build-classroom.mjs (new), build-timers.mjs, stopwatch/index.html,
20-content.css, package.json, build-inline.mjs, build-sitemap.mjs, CLAUDE.md.
Verified: build + all three gates pass (3,018 pages), all 33 internal links
resolve, no overflow at 390px.

so 995 are missing one; 345 are coastal. Nothing has a clock page without sun
and moon (that direction is closed). Whether to mint the other 995 is the

### 2026-07-31 — A passed date now fails the build
Owner: the countdown directory was still listing the finished July 19 World Cup
final among sports countdowns, and the real problem isn't that one date — it's
that a site whose whole job is counting down couldn't tell an event had
happened. Checked the current build first: the only "July 19, 2026" left
anywhere is the correct historical sentence on the 2030 page ("They beat
Argentina 1-0 … on July 19, 2026"); /countdown/'s sports card, the category
hubs, search-index.json and home-views.json are all clean. So what was seen was
the deploy lag on the earlier fix, not a surviving listing.
That is not a satisfying answer, because nothing PREVENTED it. check-dates has
found these for months — it just emailed a human and exited 0, so every rebuild
in between happily republished the page. Now `check-dates --gate` runs inside
`npm run build`, right after roll-dates (so it audits the rolled state) and
before the generators (so it fails in seconds, not after 3,000 pages). It fails
ONLY on dates that have actually passed — one-off `once` in the past, an
exhausted `dates:` table, a curated link showing a gone date. Everything else
check-dates knows ("this table is thin", "verify this estimate") stays advisory
and never blocks a deploy; a countdown to a day that is gone is not advice.
Same contract as check-pages: the build aborts and the last good deploy keeps
serving. Verified both ways — passes today, and setting the Super Bowl back to
its February date aborts the build naming both the page and the curated link.
Files: check-dates.mjs, package.json, CLAUDE.md.

### 2026-07-31 — World clock: search instead of add-a-city, cards link out, astro strip
Owner's redesign. (1) The "＋ Add city" box is gone. It answered "what time is
it there" with a card that existed only on that device, had no URL and couldn't
be shared; the same question now lands on a real page that also carries the
zone, offset, DST state and the sun and moon for that city. In its place a
search box over exactly the 109 cities that HAVE a page (baked index, so a
result can never point at a page that doesn't exist), with arrow-key selection
and Enter to go. Removed with it: makeCard/addCity, the wc_cities localStorage
list, the remove-x, and their CSS. (2) Every grid card is now a link to its city
page — a stretched-link overlay rather than wrapping the card in an <a>, because
the card also holds its own links and nested anchors are invalid. Verified by
hit-testing: a click anywhere on the card body resolves to the city link, while
a chip click still wins. (3) Cards carry compact Sun / Moon / Tides chips, and
the city pages carry the FULL astroStrip from crosslinks.mjs — the same
component the sun, moon and tide pages use, so a reader crossing families meets
the wording and framing they already know.
COASTAL: the chips ask a different question than the strip does. The coastal map
is built station -> nearest sun city, so `sunToStations` only answers "which
stations chose this city" — right for the reciprocal strip, wrong here: the Los
Angeles station sits down at San Pedro and picked a closer city, leaving LA with
no tide link at all. The chips use the curated pair when it exists and otherwise
compute the nearest station within the same 35 miles, so LA now reaches Santa
Monica while Denver and Chicago correctly get nothing. These are one-way links
out of the astronomy triangle, so no reciprocity invariant depends on them.
KNOWN GAP: 11 of the 109 cities have no /sun/ or /moon/ page (they aren't in
cities.mjs) so their cards show no chips — Pago Pago, Anchorage, Halifax, St.
John's, Azores, Tehran, Kabul, Dhaka, Adelaide, Noumea, Apia, all tier-1 offset
representatives. Adding them to CITY_DB would create 22 zero-maintenance pages
and complete the set; left as the owner's call rather than expanding two other
families unasked. Files: build-world-clock.mjs, 14-worldclock.css, CLAUDE.md.
Verified: build + both gates pass; headless run over HTTP confirms search ->
/world-clock/berlin/, card-body click -> the city page, chip click -> /sun/...,
and no horizontal overflow at 390px.

### 2026-07-31 — Retired the SpaceX launch countdowns
Owner's call: a per-pad "next launch" page has to be refreshed every few days to
stay true, and that upkeep isn't worth it. Removed all FOUR launch countdowns —
Cape Canaveral, Kennedy, Vandenberg AND Starship. Starship isn't frequent, so it
doesn't share the stated reason, but it was the same `launchSite` mechanism
fed by the same API: keeping it would have meant keeping update-spacex.mjs and
the whole fragile pipeline alive for one page, and that page would then be the
only thing able to go silently stale. Easy to restore from git if it's wanted
back on its own terms.
Gone: the 4 events + their curated astronomy links, the 4 page directories,
seo/tools/update-spacex.mjs and its build step, the launchSite branches in
build-events (the "Watch the live stream" CTA and the launch-video facade — both
existed only for these pages, and no other event sets `live` or `video`), and
the two launch types in check-volatile's MOVABLE_TYPES. The 4 URLs 301 to
/astronomy-countdowns/, which still has the solar eclipse, Perseids and Geminids.
NOTE: this also removes the site's only build-time dependency on an external API
(Launch Library 2), so the build no longer has a step that can fail on someone
else's rate limit. `npm run check` is now completely clean — "OK: all dates
current", the first time in this session's memory. Files: events.json,
popular-countdowns.json, package.json, build-events.mjs, check-volatile.mjs,
_redirects, CLAUDE.md. Verified: build + both gates pass (2,995 pages, 2,985
sitemap URLs); no reference to spacex/starship/launchSite survives anywhere in
the sources or the emitted HTML.

### 2026-07-31 — Stale sports countdowns: World Cup → 2030, last-edition results
The 2026 World Cup is over, so /world-cup-countdown/ was counting down to a date
eleven days past. Repointed to the 2030 centenary tournament: it opens 8 June
2030 at Estadio Centenario in Montevideo — the ground that staged the 1930 final
— with centenary matches in Uruguay, Argentina and Paraguay on 8–9 June before
the main hosts (Morocco, Portugal, Spain) start on 13–14 June. It counts down to
the OPENING, not the final: FIFA has announced the 8 June – 21 July window but
not the match schedule, so the final's date is still only expected, and a
countdown to an unconfirmed date is the same sin as a countdown to a past one.
Rewrote intro/sections/facts/howToWatch (the Fox/Telemundo deal ran through 2026
and 2030 rights aren't awarded), kept the Pickles story, added Spain to the
champions list.
NEW RULE, now in CLAUDE.md: when a sports countdown rolls to its next edition,
carry the last one's participants and result in the page's own PROSE, not just
the champions table — the page someone lands on the day after a final is the one
counting down to the next. Applied to the two 2026 sports events still ahead:
/world-series/ gained "How the 2025 World Series finished" (Dodgers over Blue
Jays in 7; Game 7 5-4 in 11 innings at Rogers Centre, Yamamoto MVP, first repeat
champion in 25 years) and /nfl-kickoff/ gained "How the 2025 opener went"
(Eagles 24-20 Cowboys, 65-minute lightning delay). Also fixed a real data bug:
the curated hub link had the 2026 NFL opener on Sept 10, the rich page on Sept 9
— the NFL has it as Wednesday Sept 9 (Patriots at Seahawks, a Super Bowl LX
rematch), so the hub was showing the wrong date.
NOT DONE — the three SpaceX launch countdowns. Those pages are auto-managed by
update-spacex.mjs from the Launch Library API at build time; their committed
dates are only a fallback. I could not verify current launch dates (this sandbox
has no outbound network — the API returned 403 here, and search summaries mixed
several missions), and inventing a launch date on a page whose entire promise is
the next launch is worse than leaving it. See the open question below.
Files: seo/_data/events.json, popular-countdowns.json, CLAUDE.md. Verified:
build + both gates pass; check-dates down from 8 findings to 3 (all SpaceX).

### 2026-07-31 — IndexNow: pending key verification is not a failure
Both IndexNow runs had failed, red X on the repo: `403
SiteVerificationNotCompleted`. Not a bug in the payload — a new key is not live
the moment its file is, Bing has to fetch and verify it first, and the very
first run raced its own deploy (the key file only became reachable when
Cloudflare published the commit that added it). The script treated every
non-2xx as fatal, which meant a self-resolving condition looked like a break
AND could only be retried by pushing another sitemap change. Now: a 403 whose
body says SiteVerificationNotCompleted logs what happened, records NOTHING (so
the same URLs go again next time) and exits clean; every other non-2xx still
fails loudly, because recording a submission that never happened would strand
those URLs forever. Added a daily cron to the workflow so the retry doesn't
wait on a push. Also, now that world-clock tier 2 exists, the local-time line
resolves THIS CITY's page before its zone's — Seattle was being sent to Los
Angeles (both America/Los_Angeles, tier-1 city owns the zone) despite having a
page of its own — and the anchor names what's actually at the other end:
"Seattle's time zone and UTC offset" when the city has its own page, "Pacific
Daylight Time on the world clock" when the link lands on the zone
representative instead. Files: indexnow.mjs, indexnow.yml, localtime.mjs,
wc-cities.mjs. Verified: build + both gates pass; dry run queues 2,989 URLs.
Open: the first successful submission still depends on Bing finishing
verification — watch the next scheduled run.

### 2026-07-31 — Tide titles, world-clock tier 2, timer hub description
Three owner decisions, actioned. (1) TIDE TITLES, all 143 station + destination
pages (no holdout — the owner asked for the whole set if the direction was
right, and it is): "<City>, ST Tide Times Today — High, Low & Tide Chart"
replaces "<City>, ST Tide Chart — Today's High, Low & King Tides". The old title
never contained the words "tide times", which is how the query is most often
typed, and spent its scarce characters on king tides — a real feature but a far
rarer search. "Tide chart" survives in the tail, so nothing is lost. Candidates
ladder longest-first, first fit ≤62 chars wins; every one of the 143 fits, the
longest (Carmel-by-the-Sea, Clearwater Beach) landing exactly on 62 by dropping
a comma clause. BOUNDARY DATE FOR MEASUREMENT: 2026-07-31 — tide CTR before this
(2.18% cluster, 5.77% on /tides/near-me/) is not comparable with after until
Bing re-crawls and displays the new titles. (2) WORLD CLOCK TIER 2: 79 more
cities (Toronto, Berlin, Singapore, Hong Kong, Seoul, Mexico City, Melbourne…),
109 pages in total. They share tier-1 offsets, so they stay OUT of the hub grid
— its premise is one card per offset and Toronto beside New York is the same
clock twice — and are linked from a "Current time in other cities" list instead.
Defined by name against cities.mjs, so zone and coordinates come from the table
the sun/moon pages use and cannot drift (and every tier-2 city has a sun and
moon page). Bonus: the sun/moon/tide local-time line now resolves Toronto,
Berlin, Rome etc. to their OWN world-clock page instead of the zone
representative. Fixed a real bug while wiring it — `new Map(pairs)` let the LAST
duplicate win, which handed America/New_York to a tier-2 city. (3) TIMER HUB:
title deliberately left alone (best-converting cluster, already leads with the
exact query, the proposed rewrite is a synonym swap); description refreshed —
it said "from your phone", narrowing a tool that works anywhere, and omitted the
per-timer alarm sound and the browser-tab countdown. The recommended wording
claimed keyboard controls, which the HUB does not have (the duration pages do),
so that claim is not made. Files: build-tides.mjs, wc-cities.mjs,
build-world-clock.mjs, build-timers.mjs. Verified: build + both gates pass
(2,999 pages, 2,989 sitemap URLs); headless run shows 30 grid cards + 79 chips,
Berlin's clock and visitor-diff correct from a Denver browser.

### 2026-07-31 — Local time on every sun, moon and tide place page
Owner's idea, and a good one: those ~2,330 pages are all about a place at a
moment, and every one of them quietly assumed you knew what time it was THERE —
the times on the page are in the place's own zone, not the reader's. Each now
carries one line under the heading: "It's 5:35 PM in Portland right now —
Pacific Daylight Time on the world clock." New shared component
`seo/tools/localtime.mjs` (baked at build, ticked to the real minute on load,
paused while the tab is hidden). The link resolves the page's IANA zone to the
`/world-clock/<city>/` page that represents it: exact zone id first, then ICU's
DST-INDEPENDENT zone name, which is what lets America/Toronto reach the Eastern
Time page and Europe/Rome reach Central European — no hand-kept alias table, and
genuinely distinct zones (Asia/Singapore) correctly fall back to the hub rather
than being forced into a near-match. Anchor text names the destination zone, so
it never promises a page about this city that doesn't exist. `LOCALTIME_JS` is
injected once by build-inline keyed off the markup, so the line and its ticker
cannot ship apart. The 30-city world-clock list moved to a side-effect-free
`wc-cities.mjs` so three generators can import it without re-running the
world-clock build. Files: localtime.mjs, wc-cities.mjs, build-world-clock.mjs,
build-sun.mjs, build-moon.mjs, build-tides.mjs, build-inline.mjs,
20-content.css, CLAUDE.md. Verified: build + both gates pass; headless run from
a Berlin browser shows the line rendering and ticking on sun, moon and tide
pages with no page errors.

### 2026-07-31 — SEO: IndexNow, world-clock city pages, hub metadata, near-me wording
Third batch from the Bing assessment. (1) IndexNow: `seo/tools/indexnow.mjs` +
`.github/workflows/indexnow.yml`. A URL is submitted only when its sitemap
`<lastmod>` moves past the one in `seo/_data/indexnow-state.json` — i.e. when
its generator/data actually changed — so the hourly freshness rebuild (which
commits nothing) never fires it, and we don't do the thing Bing warns about.
Public key file `3ad13cdd….txt` at the root, key mirrored in `site.json`; the
script refuses to run if the two disagree, and treats any non-200/202 as a
failure rather than recording a submission that never happened. Nothing was
submitted from here — no outbound network in the sandbox; the first real run is
the workflow, on merge. (2) `/world-clock/<city>/` for all 30 hub zones: live
clock, zone name + IANA id, UTC offset, whether DST is in effect (derived by
comparing January/July offsets, so it's right in the southern hemisphere too),
today's sun times, and a six-city comparison table. The clock time is
deliberately kept out of the title, description and prose — a minute-stale time
in a snippet is a wrong answer — so only day-stable facts are written down.
Hub cards now link to their city page. (3) Hub metadata per the assessment:
homepage, alarm, world clock, moon and sun hubs + /sun/my-location/ ("Sunrise &
Sunset Near Me" — 11k impressions at position 6.2 on a title that never said
"near me"). The TIMER hub was left alone on purpose: it is the best-converting
cluster in the export and there is no reason to churn it. (4) The homepage had a
card for every core section except the world clock — added one (4 baked city
times, each linking to its page) plus a tagline link. (5) Home sun/moon cards:
"See My Location" → "Sun times near me" / "Moon times near me", matching the
tide card and how it's searched. Files: indexnow.mjs, indexnow.yml, site.json,
build-world-clock.mjs, build-inline.mjs, build-sitemap.mjs, build-home.mjs,
build-alarm.mjs, build-sun.mjs, build-moon.mjs, 14-worldclock.css, CLAUDE.md.
Verified: build + both gates pass (2,920 pages, 2,910 sitemap URLs); headless
run confirms the city clock ticks, the 12/24h toggle works and the visitor-diff
line resolves against a foreign zone. Open: reciprocal world-clock links from
the sun/moon city pages, tide city-title A/B test (the assessment wants it on a
subset — needs a decision on which).

### 2026-07-24 — Stopwatch: shareable lap-results image (for a coach/partner)
Added a "Share" button (shows "Save image" where the Web Share API isn't
available) that builds a clean, branded PNG of the session — big total time, the
Fastest/Slowest/Average tiles, and the full lap table (chosen label noun, Lap
time + cumulative Total time, fastest green / slowest red) — on a <canvas>, then
navigator.share({files}) on mobile (native sheet → Messages/WhatsApp/email) or a
PNG download on desktop. "alarm-clock.org/stopwatch" is baked into the footer so
every shared image points back (honest organic reach). Image built synchronously

ft. Plus low tides, heights, a live NOAA chart and king tides."), with correct

### 2026-07-23 — Fail-safe build gate: never publish an empty page (#6)
Added seo/tools/check-pages.mjs as the final step of `npm run build`. The hourly
rebuild.yml only pings a Cloudflare deploy hook; Cloudflare runs the build and
publishes ONLY on exit 0 — so a post-build check that exits non-zero aborts the
deploy and the last good deployment keeps serving (Cloudflare default). This is
the implementable half of #6 (the Bing zero-doc-size investigation needs
Webmaster Tools access I don't have). The gate walks the whole output tree and,
per page, requires: >500 B, a closing </html> (not truncated), a non-empty
<title>; and for INDEXABLE pages (has canonical, not noindex) also real body text
(≥120 visible chars) + a non-empty meta description + <h1>. noindex utility pages
(404, retired /c.html & /widget/ notices) are held only to the not-empty/not-
truncated bar so they don't false-fail. Verified: passes all 1658 current pages,
and catches an injected empty-body page + a truncated file (exit 1 both). Wired
into package.json build (after build-inline). NOTE: relies on Cloudflare's build
command being `npm run build` — see the reply for the one thing to confirm.
Files: check-pages.mjs (new), package.json, CLAUDE.md. Deployed to main.

### 2026-07-22 — Tide-curve plateau fix + bake remaining home-page blanks
(1) tideDayChart flat-lined before the first tide and after the last (return
ev[0].v / ev[last].v), so the home + station charts plateaued at the ends
instead of curving toward the next tide. Fixed by padding the events with a
synthetic opposite extreme ~one tide-interval before the first and after the
last (tides alternate high<->low), so the ends interpolate. Verified LA + Boston
from real data. (2) Home-page blanks: the mini countdown cards (.cd-days) and
the work-countdown teasers (home-wk-friday/holiday/longweekend, were "—") now
bake a build-time snapshot (cd-days = day-granular "in Nd"; work = UTC-based
"Nd Nh"); the client scripts still correct to the visitor's local time on load.
Files: tide-curve.mjs, build-home.mjs. Audit note — remaining known pre-JS
blanks to SSR next: the /sun/ annual chart (#sun-year), and the tide station
cards #td-king (king tides), #td-sun (sun & moon) and #td-cur (current level).
World clock is already fully SSR'd (baked times, not --:--).

### 2026-07-22 — Batch NOAA tide fetch (decouple from hourly builds)
Hourly builds were re-fetching all ~101 stations' hi/lo from NOAA every build
(~2,470 req/day of identical, day-stable data) — the kind of per-IP load NOAA
throttles. Fixed by batching, per NOAA's own guidance (hi/lo allows up to a year
per request; space out calls). New `seo/tools/fetch-tides.mjs` fetches a 45-day
hi/lo window per station in ONE request each (200ms sleep between, retry/backoff
on 429/5xx, keeps prior data on failure) and writes `seo/_data/tide-predictions.json`.
New `.github/workflows/fetch-tides.yml` runs it WEEKLY (Mondays) and commits the
file if changed. build-tides.mjs + build-home.mjs now READ today's hi/lo from
that file (filtered by each station's local date) instead of calling NOAA — so
the hourly builds make ZERO NOAA calls, and NOAA is hit ~100x/week, spaced out.
Committed an empty placeholder JSON so builds fall back gracefully (tables/charts
omitted or schematic) until the first fetch runs. Verified end-to-end with a mock
file: 2/2 home charts + station tables bake with real times; empty file → clean
fallback, no crash. Files: fetch-tides.mjs (new), fetch-tides.yml (new),
seo/_data/tide-predictions.json (new placeholder), build-tides.mjs, build-home.mjs.
DEPENDENCY (owner): fetch-tides.yml pushes to main — if main has branch
protection blocking the Actions bot, allow it (or the file never refreshes).
Trigger it once after deploy to populate real data immediately.
Note: sun times need no API at all (pure SunCalc math), so they have no such
concern.

### 2026-07-22 — Hourly rebuilds, cache-TTL fix, proper home tide chart
Three things.
(1) Cache: the "cache won't clear without a manual Cloudflare purge" issue was
`s-maxage=86400` in _headers — a Pages deploy purges the build cache but NOT
the zone CDN cache, so HTML sat at the edge up to 24h. Lowered to `s-maxage=3600`
so the edge self-refreshes within ~1h even without a purge.
(2) Cadence: rebuild.yml now runs HOURLY (`20 * * * *`) instead of the 3 6-hour
marks, so the whole site is near-real-time native HTML. ~24 builds/day ≈ 720/mo,
which EXCEEDS Cloudflare's free 500 — requires the Workers Paid plan ($5/mo,
5,000 builds). Owner opted in.
(3) Home tide charts: replaced the minimal renderer (which read as "only highs")
with a new shared `tideDayChart()` in tide-curve.mjs that matches the tides-page
chart style — dark panel, ft gridlines + labels, hour axis, cosine-interpolated
curve, and BOTH yellow-high / grey-low markers labelled with height + time. Real
NOAA data on Cloudflare (fetched at build), schematic fallback when NOAA is
unreachable (sandbox). Verified the renderer with mock data + a headless shot.
Files: _headers, .github/workflows/rebuild.yml, seo/tools/tide-curve.mjs
(new tideDayChart), seo/tools/build-home.mjs (use it, drop the inline renderer).
NEXT (owner request): SSR this tide chart + the sun dial on ALL location pages
(/tides/<station>/ and /sun/<city>/) so they never paint blank — the tide data
is already fetched at build (build-tides fetchTodayTides); the sun dial needs a
Node port of the DIAL_JS drawing.

### 2026-07-22 — Rebuild every 6h + "days until" in event/celeb meta descriptions
Two freshness changes riding the scheduled Cloudflare rebuild.
(1) Cadence: added rebuild.yml crons at 05:20/17:20/23:20 UTC so — with
maintenance.yml's 11:20 run — the whole site rebuilds every 6 hours (4×/day,
~120 builds/mo, well under Cloudflare's free 500). Each deploy also purges the
edge cache, so sun/tide/countdown "today" values stay fresh to within ~6h for
every timezone, on each crawl and each visit. Rebuild-only workflow (no audit/
report); warns if CF_DEPLOY_HOOK is unset.
(2) SEO snippets: build-events.mjs now leads each event/celeb page's
`<meta name="description">` with the baked "N days until <name> — <weekday>,
<date>." count (e.g. "156 days until Christmas — Friday, December 25, 2026. …"),
then as much of the evergreen desc as fits under ~157 chars. Refreshed every
rebuild, so the SERP snippet shows the current number. og:/twitter: descriptions
stay evergreen (the OG image already carries the live date); the title stays
stable (a live number in the title invites Google to rewrite it). The on-page
`#cd-days-away` count was already baked + JS-hydrated; this extends the freshness
to the search snippet. Files: .github/workflows/rebuild.yml, build-events.mjs.
Verified: christmas/taylor-swift/anniversary snippets lead with the count, og
stayed evergreen, length in range.
Cost note: rebuilds are free on Cloudflare Pages + GitHub Actions at this scale.
Cloudflare "Deployment failed" notification (dashboard → Notifications) is the
owner-side step for build-failure alerts.

### 2026-07-22 — /sun/ city pages: locally-computed seasonal facts + annual note
Follow-up to the SSR keystone: added the review's Priority 6 (calculated local
context) and the rest of Priority 1's bake list as crawlable text. New Node
helpers in build-sun.mjs: `sunDecl` (solar declination), `riseSetDir` (compass
direction of sunrise/sunset from declination + latitude), `nextSolarEvent`
(nearest equinox/solstice, computed ONCE for the build since it's
latitude-independent) and `seasonName` (hemisphere-correct — a September event
is "autumn equinox" in the north, "spring equinox" in the south). `sunSSR` now
also scans the year for the longest/shortest day and a smoothed daily trend.
Each city page gets a baked "Daylight & seasons in <city>" card, e.g. Las Vegas:
"The sun rises in the east-northeast and sets in the west-northwest today. Days
are getting shorter by about 1 minute a day right now. Las Vegas gets about
4 hours 56 minutes more daylight on its longest day than its shortest. The next
autumn equinox is 64 days away, on September 23." Also baked the annual-chart
note ("Longest day: June 22 — 14 h 37 m … Shortest day: December 21 — 9 h 42 m").
These are build-stable (refreshed by the scheduled rebuild), distinct from the
JS-live answer sentence. Small `.sun-facts` CSS added. Files: seo/tools/
build-sun.mjs, assets/css/style.css. Verified: matches the review's example
figures; southern-hemisphere cities correctly flip the season name; inlined JS
still passes node --check. Still deferred: SSR the annual SVG itself, monthly
calendar, golden/blue hour, hub/state redesigns.

### 2026-07-22 — /sun/ city pages: server-render the answer into the HTML (SEO)
Biggest problem from the ChatGPT SEO review: the ~1,000 city pages shipped
crawlable HTML with "—" placeholders, a blank date, an empty 7-day table and
empty chart — the real values only appeared after client JS ran, so to Google
the pages looked like near-duplicate placeholders. Fixed the keystone (the
review's step 1–2) on the location pages:
- Ported the client sunCalc solar math to a Node function in build-sun.mjs
  (`nSunCalc` + Intl formatters) and added `sunSSR(city)` computing, for a
  single BUILD_NOW instant, each city's first light / sunrise / solar noon /
  sunset / last light / day length, a filled 7-day table, and the daylight
  change vs yesterday — all in the city's own time zone.
- Rewrote the intro answer-first: "Sunrise in Las Vegas is 5:40 AM and sunset
  is 7:55 PM on Tuesday, July 21, 2026. Las Vegas has 14 hours 16 minutes of
  daylight today — 1 minute less than yesterday. First light begins at 5:11 AM,
  and last light ends at 8:25 PM." Added a "Pacific Daylight Time · Calculated
  for central <city>" line. These now exist in the page source.
- Baked the stat rows and the 7-day table into the static HTML (no more "—").
- The existing client JS still recomputes everything for the visitor's exact
  current date on load (extended renderFor to also update the answer spans,
  the long date, and the delta), so the baked values are a correct, indexable
  default that JS enhances — the page is now useful with JS disabled.
Scope: location pages only; /sun/anywhere/ (noindex, JS-located) unchanged.
Deferred to follow-ups (the review's later steps): SSR the annual chart, sun
direction + next solstice/equinox, monthly calendar, golden/blue hour, and the
state/hub redesigns. Files: seo/tools/build-sun.mjs. Verified: Las Vegas +
London pages ship real values in source; math matches real-world times; 7-day
table = 8 rows; inlined JS passes `node --check`.
DEPENDENCY (owner): baked values are as-of the last build, so a scheduled
daily rebuild (Cloudflare Pages cron deploy, or a GitHub Actions cron running
`npm run build` + commit) is needed to keep the crawled date fresh. Not set up
yet — flagged to the owner.

### 2026-07-21 — Fix CSP console errors from Cloudflare analytics (privacy-lean)
PageSpeed on /timer/15-minutes/ logged three CSP console errors — all the
site's own analytics being blocked: the Cloudflare Web Analytics beacon
(static.cloudflareinsights.com/beacon.min.js) and GA4's advertising pings
(stats.g.doubleclick.net + www.google.com/ads/ga-audiences). Main GA is
unaffected — it runs first-party through Cloudflare Zaraz (/cdn-cgi/zaraz/,
already covered by 'self'); only Google Signals/remarketing escapes to Google
directly. Chose the privacy-lean fix: allowlist ONLY Cloudflare Web Analytics
in the CSP (`script-src` + `connect-src` gain static.cloudflareinsights.com,
`connect-src` also gains cloudflareinsights.com for the beacon POST), and the
GA advertising domains are intentionally NOT added — the owner disables Google
Signals / Advertising Features in GA4 (Admin → Data Settings → Data Collection)
so those pings stop at the source. File: _headers (static config, not built).
Deploy needed for the CSP to take effect (Cloudflare serves the header).
Other Lighthouse items on the page were reviewed and left as intentional
tradeoffs: /api/geo (async privacy-banner geo-check), the 640×640 timer image
(retina 2x + the SEO thumbnail), inlined "unused" CSS (no-render-blocking
architecture), and Zaraz script cache TTL (Cloudflare-served under /cdn-cgi/).

### 2026-07-21 — Timer search thumbnails: expose the square dial images to crawlers
The per-duration square dial illustrations (`/assets/img/timer-*.webp`, 640×640)
were on-page decoration only — nothing in the `<head>` told a search engine they
represented the page, so they never surfaced as SERP/image thumbnails (esp. Bing).
Three fixes: (1) build-inline now injects `<meta name="robots"
content="max-image-preview:large">` on every indexable page that doesn't already
declare a robots meta (noindex pages and the events/420 pages that already set it
are left untouched) — the main lever for large thumbnails in Google & Bing; (2)
`appLd()` gained an optional `image` field, and build-timers passes each duration
page's square webp (absolute URL) into the WebApplication JSON-LD — the explicit
"use this image" signal Bing recommends; (3) duration pages with a square webp now
emit it as a SECOND og:image (the wide 1200×630 `/api/og` dial card stays primary
for the 1.91:1 social ratio), so engines can pick either for the thumbnail. Pages
above 60 min (65–90) that have a webp but no wide card now carry the square as their
sole og:image + structured image instead of the generic injected card. Files:
seo/tools/lib.mjs, seo/tools/build-timers.mjs, seo/tools/build-inline.mjs. Verified
in built output: 5-min page has wide+square og:image + JSON-LD image + robots meta;
90-min page square-only; hub/noindex/events pages behave correctly (no dupes).
Next: request a recrawl in Bing Webmaster Tools (can take weeks); optionally export
larger (≥1200px) dial images for a stronger "large" preview.

Extracted the timer's notification code into shared seo/tools/notify.mjs
(NOTIFY_SW + window.acNotify.init/show/clear). Wired it into the alarm clock:

when saving an alarm or tapping Test; build-alarm.mjs writes /alarm-clock/sw.js
(scope covers the about + per-time pages) and includes NOTIFY_JS, same for

now comes from NOTIFY_SW). Added a subtle "🔔 Notify me when time's up" opt-in

default) and, on iPhone, points to Add to Home Screen (no web notifications in a
Safari tab). Files: notify.mjs (new), build-timers.mjs, alarm-widget.mjs,
build-alarm.mjs, build-alarm-times.mjs, style.css. Verified in browser: timer
notification fires with Stop, hint states render, alarm registers its SW at
/alarm-clock/ on save, no JS errors.

### 2026-07-08 — Timers: system notification (with Stop) when time's up
When a timer finishes it now posts a system notification to the OS notification
shade so it's easy to silence — title "⏰ Time's up!", requireInteraction, and a
Stop action button. New notifications-only service worker at /timer/sw.js
(scope /timer/, NO fetch handler → no caching/staleness): it shows the Stop
button and relays a tap from the shade back to the page (postMessage
'ac-timer-stop') to stop the in-page alarm; degrades to a plain Notification
(tap-to-focus) where SW/actions aren't available. Permission is requested on
Start (user gesture); notification cleared when the alarm is stopped. Platform
reality: works in a normal tab on Android + desktop; on iPhone ONLY when the
site is added to the Home Screen (iOS Safari tabs can't show web
notifications), and only while the page is still alive (no backend push).
Scoped to timers for now (this thread's context); alarm clock could get the

### 2026-07-08 — Kill Cloudflare's email-decode.min.js (PageSpeed find)
PageSpeed flagged /cdn-cgi/scripts/.../cloudflare-static/email-decode.min.js as
a render-blocking third-party script on the 420 page. It's injected by
Cloudflare's Email Address Obfuscation whenever a raw email appears in the
served HTML — here the only trigger was the placeholder "you@example.com" on
the site-wide "Share an idea" email input (and the same string on the create
template + the report/suggest-event/promote-event/wrong-date forms). Replaced
every one with "Your email address" so no raw email pattern reaches the edge →
Cloudflare stops obfuscating and no longer injects the script on any page.
Inputs keep type="email"; no functional change. Files: build-inline.mjs,
seo/templates/create.html, report/, suggest-event/, promote-event/,
wrong-date/ index.html. Verified: zero raw emails in served HTML site-wide, no
JS errors. NOTE: Cloudflare caches HTML — a cache purge (or waiting out the
TTL) may be needed before PageSpeed shows it gone.

### 2026-07-08 — 420 pages: zero-state smoke, dynamic share text, green OG
Implemented the legitimate, effective parts of an SEO/virality request for the
420 pages (and declined the spammy parts — see note):
- **Zero-state animation:** when the countdown hits 4:20 it now fires a rising
  green smoke haze over the display (CSS, clipped to the stage, respects
  prefers-reduced-motion) alongside the existing confetti + "done" message.
  Refactored the zero handler to fire once per event (was re-firing every second
  at zero).

  swaps to "It's 4:20. You know what time it is." (annual: "It's 4/20…"), then
  reverts. Real people click those links, so this is worthwhile (unlike JS-
  updating og: tags, which crawlers never see).

  pointed the 420 og:image/twitter:image at it (vibrant green 1200x630), plus
  robots:max-image-preview:large, og:url, og:image dims/alt, twitter title/desc.
- **Declined (and told the owner why):** keyword-stuffed ARIA labels (cloaking +
  hurts screen-reader users), keyword-stuffed class names/IDs (not a ranking
  signal), EXIF stuffing (no static file; ignored), and JS-rewriting og: tags
  (invisible to scrapers).

  Cloudflare (theme is additive + falls back to generic, so it can't break other

### 2026-07-07 — New feature: evergreen "escapism" micro-countdown pages
Owner asked for a new SEO content vertical targeting bored-at-your-desk
searches ("countdown to 5pm", "countdown to Friday", "next long weekend",
"how many days until the next federal holiday") — pages people bookmark and
leave open on a second monitor rather than one-off shared events, plus a full
screen mode like the alarm clock's and two new homepage cards.

**Four new pages**, all client-side computed so the daily/weekly ones can
never go stale (no build-time date baked in at all):
- `/countdown-to-5pm/` — next 5:00 PM, visitor's own local time (daily).
- `/countdown-to-friday/` — next 5:00 PM Friday, doubling as the "weekend
  countdown clock" keyword (weekly).
- `/next-federal-holiday/` — whichever of the 11 official US federal
  holidays is soonest, auto-advancing once one passes.
- `/next-long-weekend/` — the next federal holiday landing on a Friday/Monday
  (or Thanksgiving), targeting 5 PM on the last workday before the break
  actually starts (Monday holiday → the Friday before; Thanksgiving → the
  Wednesday before).

**New federal holiday data + date math** (`seo/_data/federal-holidays.json`,
`upcomingFederalHolidays()` in `lib.mjs`): all 11 official holidays — the 8
already in `events.json` plus Juneteenth, Veterans Day, and Columbus Day,
which weren't tracked anywhere on the site yet. Reuses the existing
`nextOccurrence()` date math as-is (no new recurrence engine needed) and adds
the standard federal Saturday→Friday / Sunday→Monday observed-date shift.
Bakes 3 years of occurrences per holiday into each page so both pages stay
correct for years even between rebuilds, not just until the next daily
maintenance run.

**Shared fullscreen widget** (`seo/tools/microevent-widget.mjs`): computes
the target purely from the visitor's own clock, ticks every second, and
auto-advances to the next occurrence the instant it hits zero (celebrates
with the existing `effects.js` confetti burst) — so a tab left open across
5pm or midnight Friday rolls straight into the next countdown instead of
freezing like a one-off event page would. Full screen reuses the alarm
clock's exact pattern (exit button, screen wake lock, hourly anti-burn-in
nudge) since leaving a tab open unattended for hours is the same use case the
bedside clock was already built for.

**Two new homepage cards** ("Countdown to Friday", "Next Federal Holiday")
with live mini-previews, ticking once a minute. Caught and fixed a real bug
while building these: initially wrapped each card in `<a href=...>` with a
nested `<a class="tc-open-btn">` "Open" button inside — invalid nested-anchor
HTML that rendered as a visible layout glitch (a ghost button shape). Fixed
by switching to the same `<div data-href=...>` + JS click-delegation pattern
the existing Timer/Stopwatch cards already use for exactly this reason.

Verified with `wrangler pages dev` (real Functions) + Playwright: all four
pages tick down correctly and count seconds accurately; fullscreen genuinely
enters/exits (`document.fullscreenElement`, `mc-fs` body class, exit button)
with zero console errors; both home cards show correct live values, link
correctly, and both click paths (the "Open" button and the rest of the card)
navigate correctly after the nested-anchor fix. Ran a full Lighthouse pass
too, since this session already put a lot of work into the site's PageSpeed
scores: all four new pages score 100/100/100/100 with zero CLS; the home
page's minor 99/96 blip on one run traced to the pre-existing World Clock
card and sandbox network noise (re-ran clean at 100/100), not anything the
new cards introduced. `npm run check` clean apart from the two pre-existing
SpaceX findings.

### 2026-07-06 — Lazy-load the home page's search index + trending views map
Owner asked to make sure JS is only loaded on pages that need it, ideally
split per page/feature. Audited actual byte payloads first rather than
guessing: the site already does almost all of this — each tool page
(alarm/timer/stopwatch/world-clock) ships only its own dedicated JS from its
own generator, event pages get just the `effects` module (7.9 KB), and fully
static pages (terms, privacy, calendar, popular, hubs, 404) ship **zero**
bundled JS. The only truly universal bits are the nav dropdown's click-
outside handler (163 B) and the site-wide "share an idea" box (~1 KB) — both
deliberate, tiny, sitewide features, not leftover bloat.

The one real finding: the home page's ~30 KB of "JS" was mostly **data**, not
code — `window.AC_INDEX` (11.4 KB, the full site search index) and
`window.AC_VIEWS` (12 KB, a site-wide view-id→label map for the trending
card) were both embedded inline in `build-home.mjs`'s `doc()` template,
shipped on every load of `/` and `/countdown/` whether or not a visitor ever
touches search or scrolls to the trending card.

Fixed: both are now written to static JSON files at build time
(`assets/data/search-index.json`, `assets/data/home-views.json`) instead of
inlined:
- **Search index**: the search script now fetches it lazily — on first
  `focus`/keystroke in the search box — instead of on every page load.
  Buffers the in-flight query so typing before the fetch resolves still
  renders correctly once it lands.
- **Views map**: fetched in parallel (`Promise.all`) with the existing
  `/api/trending` call the "Trending" card already makes, instead of being
  baked into the HTML. This doesn't reduce bytes for a typical visit (the
  card populates on every home-page load regardless), but it moves ~12 KB out
  of the initial HTML document — smaller parse/compile weight, and the file
  is now a separately cacheable static asset instead of re-downloading
  identical data inline on every single page view.

Net result: the home page's inline script payload dropped from 30,243 bytes
to 10,177 bytes (~66% smaller) for a visitor who never touches search; search
itself now costs nothing until actually used.

Verified with `wrangler pages dev` (real Functions) + a real Playwright pass
on both `/` and `/countdown/` (which share this same code path): confirmed
`search-index.json` is *not* requested until the search box is focused,
confirmed `home-views.json` *is* requested on load (parallel with the
trending fetch), confirmed the trending card still populates with correct
resolved labels/counts, and confirmed searching still returns correct results
("taylor" → Taylor Swift, "christmas" → Christmas 2026) on both pages. No
console errors beyond the sandbox's own network block on Google Analytics'
external domain (pre-existing, unrelated). `npm run check` clean apart from
the two pre-existing SpaceX findings.

### 2026-07-06 — Lighthouse pass to 100/100 across every page type + closed the one open PR
Owner asked to close all open items and push every page toward a near-perfect
PageSpeed/Lighthouse score, with CLS and performance called out specifically.
Ran Lighthouse (mobile, headless Chromium) against every distinct page type —
home, alarm clock, timer (hub + a duration page), stopwatch, world clock,
countdown hub, a curated event page, calendar, popular, a category hub, create
(+ an occasion variant), an alarm-time landing page, and a personalized `/c`
share link — served by `wrangler pages dev` (real KV-backed Functions, not a
bare static server) so `/api/*` calls behave like production. Found and fixed
three real, root-caused issues that were docking a11y/CLS on multiple pages:
- **Contrast failures (2 spots):** `.ac-dim` ("no alarms" placeholder) and
  `.ac-batt` (battery badge) both dimmed the theme's `--seg-on` red via
  `opacity`, which blends toward the near-black panel background — math
  showed that always drops red/purple below the 4.5:1 AA floor no matter how
  little you dim it (full-strength red is already only ~5.3:1). Fixed both to
  de-emphasize without touching opacity: `.ac-dim` is now italic/normal-weight
  at full color, `.ac-batt`'s opacity is dropped entirely. Full contrast at
  every theme color, still visually distinct from the emphasized text.
- **Brand-logo a11y (WCAG 2.5.3 label-in-name):** the top-left clock logo SVG
  had literal `<text>` glyphs for the 12 hour numerals inside an
  `aria-hidden="true"` wrapper with `aria-label="alarm-clock.org home"` on the
  link — axe flags this because the numerals are still visually rendered text
  that isn't part of the accessible name. Replaced the 12 `<text>` numerals
  with plain hour-mark dots (`seo/tools/build-inline.mjs`, also regenerates
  `favicon.svg` from the same SVG) — a minimal, valid icon simplification at
  48px, and removes the only real text node in scope for good.
- **CLS from async "trending/most-watched" cards:** the home page, countdown
  hub, and every category hub page render a `hidden` card that un-hides via
  JS once `/api/trending` or `/api/popular` resolves (well after first
  paint), pushing whatever renders after it down — a real, measurable layout
  shift (0.03–0.08) hitting content already in the mobile viewport. Root
  cause was DOM order: the async card sat *above* the static "Upcoming
  events" card (home/hub) or the main list (category pages). Reordered so
  the static content renders first and the async card is last — when it
  later un-hides, it only pushes content that's typically already scrolled
  past the fold, so it stops counting against CLS. No visual change to the
  populated end state, just which one appears first in the stack
  (`seo/tools/build-home.mjs`, `seo/tools/build-category-pages.mjs`).

Verified: rebuilt (`npm run build`), re-ran the full Lighthouse pass — every
tested page now scores **100/100/100/100** (performance/a11y/best-practices/
SEO) except the personalized `/c.html?...` share link, whose `seo=66` is the
intentional `noindex,follow` on personalized links (not a bug — flagged
during triage and left alone). One CLS point not chased further: `/popular/`
sits at CLS 0.044 (already well inside Google's "good" ≤0.1 band) from
reordering already-visible `<li>` rows as live ranks arrive — fixing that
properly means reworking the live-sort UX itself, out of scope for this pass.
`npm run check`'s date audit is clean (same two known SpaceX-pad findings,
still blocked on the owner supplying `LL2_API_KEY`); its link-check flagged
377 "blocked 403s" against Wikipedia/Instagram/etc., which is this sandbox's
own network egress failing raw `curl` too — an environment artifact, not a
real broken-link regression.

**Closed the one open item on GitHub:** PR #1, an auto-generated Cloudflare
"Workers autoconfig" PR adding a `wrangler.jsonc` (Workers-style, `assets.
directory`, no KV bindings). This project is deliberately Cloudflare **Pages**
(`wrangler.toml`, KV bindings for the report/view-counter Functions) — merging
would have added a conflicting second config, not helped anything. Closed
with an explanation instead of merging.

### 2026-07-06 — Closed two open questions + split the shared stylesheet per page type
- **Full-screen exit toast:** owner chose to keep the real Fullscreen API (the
